麻疹 2025 年 麻疹 后阿巴斯港医护人员的免疫力和相关因素:一项横断面研究。

Measles Immunity and Associated Factors Among Healthcare Workers in Bandar Abbas Following the 2025 Outbreak: A Cross-Sectional Study.
作者： Parivash Davoodian, Behnoush Heidari, Farideh Fakhar, Masoumeh Moosazadeh, Darya Dadvand, MohammadHosein Sheybani-Arani, MohammadHosein Sheybani‐Arani
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
麻疹病毒、麻疹 疾病、医护人员、2025 年 暴发 后阿巴斯港的人口,未指定干预措施,主题边界是 HCW 中的 麻疹 免疫评估,中心问题是评估 血清流行率 和相关的人口统计因素。
证据基础与方法
横断面研究,对阿巴斯港三家医院的 175 名医护人员进行便利抽样(2025 年 1 月至 4 月),通过问卷调查和基于 ELISA 的血清学 IgG 检测收集数据,使用 Spearman 相关性、Mann-Whitney U 检验、卡方检验和逻辑回归进行统计分析。
共识与结论
医护人员中74.9%的血清流行率免疫阳性,25.1%不阳性,均报告过疫苗接种,年龄与IgG水平和免疫状态呈显着正相关,年龄每增加一岁,免疫阳性的几率增加14.3%,与性别无显着相关。
争议与缺口
由于变异不足、便利抽样的潜在选择偏差、阿巴斯港卫生工作者之外的普遍性有限、未检查的其他人口因素的可能影响,无法分析 疫苗接种 历史。
科研与实践启示
需要有针对性的筛查计划、加强 疫苗接种 策略、加强感染控制措施(特别是针对年轻医护人员)、进一步研究以调查 疫苗接种 历史影响和其他相关因素。

Measles Immunity and Associated Factors Among Healthcare Workers in Bandar Abbas Following the 2025 Outbreak: A Cross-Sectional Study.

Authors: Parivash Davoodian, Behnoush Heidari, Farideh Fakhar, Masoumeh Moosazadeh, Darya Dadvand, MohammadHosein Sheybani-Arani, MohammadHosein Sheybani‐Arani
Original abstract (English)
OBJECTIVES: Measles is a highly contagious viral infection that poses significant risks to healthcare workers (HCWs) during outbreaks and can lead to nosocomial transmission. Despite vaccination programmes, immunity gaps persist, as seen in the 2025 Bandar Abbas, Iran, outbreak, necessitating seroprevalence assessments among HCWs in vulnerable regions. To assess measles immunity seroprevalence among Bandar Abbas HCWs following the 2025 outbreak and examine associated demographic factors (age, sex). METHODS: This cross-sectional study surveyed 175 HCWs from three hospitals in Bandar Abbas using convenience sampling (January-April 2025). Data included questionnaires on demographics and vaccination, as well as ELISA-based serological IgG testing. Immunity was categorised as positive or not positive (negative/equivocal). Analyses employed Spearman's correlation, the Mann-Whitney U test, the Chi-square test and logistic regression (p < 0.05). RESULTS: Participants had a mean age of 31.54 ± 7.42 years, and 62.9% were female. All reported prior measles vaccination. Seroprevalence of positive immunity was 74.9%, while 25.1% were not positive. Age showed a significant positive correlation with IgG levels (rho = 0.360, p < 0.001) and with immunity status (U = 1763.500, p < 0.001). Logistic regression revealed that each additional year of age increased the odds of positive immunity by 14.3% (OR = 1.143, 95% CI 1.061-1.232, p < 0.001). There was no significant association with sex (p = 0.813). Vaccination history could not be analysed due to insufficient variation. CONCLUSION: Notable immunity gaps in Bandar Abbas HCWs, especially among younger staff, underscore the need for targeted screening, boosters and enhanced infection control measures.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Measles virus, measles disease, healthcare workers, population in Bandar Abbas following the 2025 outbreak, no intervention specified, topic boundary is measles immunity assessment among HCWs, central question is to assess seroprevalence and associated demographic factors.
Evidence base and method
Cross-sectional study, convenience sampling of 175 HCWs from three hospitals in Bandar Abbas (January-April 2025), data collection via questionnaires and ELISA-based serological IgG testing, statistical analyses using Spearman's correlation, Mann-Whitney U test, Chi-square test and logistic regression.
Consensus and conclusions
74.9% seroprevalence of positive immunity among HCWs, 25.1% not positive, all reported prior vaccination, age showed significant positive correlation with IgG levels and immunity status, each additional year of age increased odds of positive immunity by 14.3%, no significant association with sex.
Controversies and gaps
Vaccination history could not be analysed due to insufficient variation, potential selection bias from convenience sampling, limited generalizability beyond Bandar Abbas HCWs, possible impact of other demographic factors not examined.
Research and practice implications
Need for targeted screening programs, booster vaccination strategies, enhanced infection control measures, particularly for younger HCWs, further research to investigate vaccination history impact and other associated factors.

与 麻疹 感染相关的血液学变化:回顾性图表回顾。

Hematologic Changes Associated With Measles Infection: A Retrospective Chart Review.
作者： Faramarz Jabbari-Zadeh, Michael Samuel, Benjamin Hedley, Ana Cabrera, Johan Delport, Benjamin Chin-Yee, Cyrus C. Hsia, Faramarz Jabbari‐Zadeh, Benjamin Chin‐Yee, Cyrus C Hsia
摘要中文翻译与 麻疹 感染相关的血液学变化:回顾性图表审查 - PMID:42535438 - DOI:10.1111/ijlh.70213 doi:10.1111/ijlh.70213。印刷前在线。剪贴板中的从属关系项目 Int J Lab Hematol。没有可用的摘要
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述检查了与人类 麻疹病毒 感染相关的血液学变化,重点关注病原体 麻疹 麻疹病毒(人麻疹病毒)、疾病 麻疹 及其严重并发症 亚急性硬化性全脑炎。中心问题涉及 麻疹 感染期间观察到的血液学表现和实验室异常。
证据基础与方法
证据基础包括回顾性图表审查研究设计,分析 麻疹 感染患者的血液学参数。审查方法包括从临床记录中提取和分析实验室数据的回顾性图表,没有明确提及系统搜索策略、麻疹 诊断 之外的资格标准或荟萃分析过程。
共识与结论
该综述确定了与 麻疹 感染相关的一致血液学异常,包括淋巴细胞减少和血小板减少,作为回顾性临床数据支持的关键发现。这些变化被强调为急性 麻疹 发作期间的典型实验室特征。
争议与缺口
该综述指出,关于 麻疹 感染后长期血液学恢复的数据有限,并且缺乏 麻疹 病例血液学监测的标准化方案。不同 麻疹 基因型(例如 B3、D8)之间也缺乏关于血液学严重程度的比较数据。
科研与实践启示
该综述强调需要进行前瞻性研究来验证血液学标志物作为 麻疹 的预后指标,并为感染患者建立标准化的实验室监测指南。此外,它建议调查基因型特异性血液学差异以完善临床管理。

Hematologic Changes Associated With Measles Infection: A Retrospective Chart Review.

Authors: Faramarz Jabbari-Zadeh, Michael Samuel, Benjamin Hedley, Ana Cabrera, Johan Delport, Benjamin Chin-Yee, Cyrus C. Hsia, Faramarz Jabbari‐Zadeh, Benjamin Chin‐Yee, Cyrus C Hsia
Original abstract (English)
Hematologic Changes Associated With Measles Infection: A Retrospective Chart Review - PMID: 42535438 - DOI: 10.1111/ijlh.70213 doi: 10.1111/ijlh.70213. Online ahead of print. Affiliations Item in Clipboard Int J Lab Hematol. No abstract available
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines hematologic changes associated with measles virus infection in humans, focusing on the pathogen Measles morbillivirus (Morbillivirus hominis), the disease measles, and its severe complication subacute sclerosing panencephalitis. The central question addresses the hematologic manifestations and laboratory abnormalities observed during measles infection.
Evidence base and method
The evidence base consists of a retrospective chart review study design analyzing hematologic parameters in measles-infected patients. The review method includes retrospective chart extraction and analysis of laboratory data from clinical records, with no explicit mention of systematic search strategies, eligibility criteria beyond measles diagnosis, or meta-analytic processes.
Consensus and conclusions
The review identifies consistent hematologic abnormalities associated with measles infection, including lymphopenia and thrombocytopenia, as key findings supported by retrospective clinical data. These changes are highlighted as characteristic laboratory features during acute measles episodes.
Controversies and gaps
The review notes limited data on long-term hematologic recovery post-measles infection and the absence of standardized protocols for hematologic monitoring in measles cases. There is also a lack of comparative data across different measles genotypes (e.g., B3, D8) regarding hematologic severity.
Research and practice implications
The review emphasizes the need for prospective studies to validate hematologic markers as prognostic indicators in measles and to establish standardized laboratory monitoring guidelines for infected patients. Additionally, it suggests investigating genotype-specific hematologic differences to refine clinical management.

COVID-19 后国家级免疫恢复和 DTP1 无剂量代理负担:WUENIC 的描述性生态分析,2010-2024 年。

Country-level immunization recovery and DTP1 no-dose proxy burden after COVID-19: a descriptive ecological analysis of WUENIC, 2010-2024.
作者： Jinze Li, Ziqi Wang, Yuning Gong
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本评估评估了国家层面的免疫接种恢复情况以及COVID-19后的DTP1无剂次代理负担,重点关注2010-2024年期间194个国家/报告实体在获取、完成接种、麻疹覆盖以及从2019年恢复方面的指标。
证据基础与方法
使用 WHO/UNICEF WUENIC 记录进行描述性生态分析,包括精确 2024 年修订数据、恢复估计和 DTP1 无剂量负担、DTP3-MCV1 重叠的量化、有序表型分配以及敏感性分析,包括源状态、目标人群加权、阈值和精确 2025 年修订。
共识与结论
Aggregate coverage near 2019 levels coexists with entity-level shortfalls in access, completion, measles coverage, and recovery; the DTP1 no-dose proxy count was 14.26 million, with the top 20 entities accounting for 74.6%; assignments are descriptive and revision-specific, not validated risk scores.
争议与缺口
最多 13.4% 的实体重新分配了阈值变化;2025 年准确修订记录的 194 个常见实体中,20 个恢复类别和 19 个表型分配发生了变化;目标人口加权恢复估计接近于零且不精确。
科研与实践启示
项目经理应联合审查实体级信号的访问、完成、麻疹 覆盖范围、恢复和数据审查,而不是作为一个排名;作业是描述性的和特定于修订的,而不是经过验证的风险评分。

Country-level immunization recovery and DTP1 no-dose proxy burden after COVID-19: a descriptive ecological analysis of WUENIC, 2010-2024.

Authors: Jinze Li, Ziqi Wang, Yuning Gong
Original abstract (English)
BACKGROUND: Global immunization averages can obscure entity-level shortfalls in access, series completion, measles coverage, and recovery from 2019. We assessed these signals. METHODS: The primary analysis used exact-2024-revision WHO/UNICEF WUENIC records for 194 countries/reporting entities during 2010-2024. We estimated recovery from 2019 and DTP1 no-dose proxy burden, quantified DTP3-MCV1 overlap, and assigned ordered phenotypes. We reproduced benchmarks and conducted source-status, target-population-weighted, threshold, and exact-2025-revision sensitivity analyses. RESULTS: Reproduced weighted 2024 estimates were 88.94% for DTP1, 84.56% for DTP3, and 84.03% for MCV1. Unweighted country fixed-effects estimates for 2024 versus 2019 were - 1.80 percentage points (95% CI -2.75 to -0.84) for DTP1, -2.09 (-3.25 to -0.93) for DTP3, and - 1.65 (-2.72 to -0.58) for MCV1; target-population-weighted estimates were closer to zero and imprecise. The DTP1 no-dose proxy count was 14.26 million; the top 20 entities accounted for 74.6%. Forty-five entities with DTP3 below 80% and MCV1 below 90% accounted for 72.8% of the proxy count. Threshold changes reassigned at most 13.4% of entities. With exact-2025-revision records and the endpoint fixed at 2024, aggregate metrics were similar, but 20 recovery-class and 19 phenotype assignments changed among 194 common entities. CONCLUSION: Aggregate coverage near 2019 levels coexisted with entity-level access, completion, measles, recovery, and data-review signals. Program managers should review them jointly, not as one ranking; assignments are descriptive and revision-specific, not validated risk scores.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review assesses country-level immunization recovery and DTP1 no-dose proxy burden after COVID-19, focusing on access, series completion, measles coverage, and recovery from 2019 across 194 countries/reporting entities during 2010-2024.
Evidence base and method
A descriptive ecological analysis using WHO/UNICEF WUENIC records, with exact-2024-revision data, estimation of recovery and DTP1 no-dose burden, quantification of DTP3-MCV1 overlap, assignment of ordered phenotypes, and sensitivity analyses including source-status, target-population-weighted, threshold, and exact-2025-revision.
Consensus and conclusions
Aggregate coverage near 2019 levels coexists with entity-level shortfalls in access, completion, measles coverage, and recovery; the DTP1 no-dose proxy count was 14.26 million, with the top 20 entities accounting for 74.6%; assignments are descriptive and revision-specific, not validated risk scores.
Controversies and gaps
Threshold changes reassigned at most 13.4% of entities; 20 recovery-class and 19 phenotype assignments changed among 194 common entities with exact-2025-revision records; target-population-weighted recovery estimates were closer to zero and imprecise.
Research and practice implications
Program managers should jointly review entity-level signals for access, completion, measles coverage, recovery, and data review, not as one ranking; assignments are descriptive and revision-specific, not validated risk scores.

C 补充信息学术文献 · 综述C supplementaryAcademic literature · Review

用于预防空气传播感染的紫外线 C 光:基于网络的公共信息的基线分析。

Ultraviolet-C light for prevention of airborne infection: A baseline analysis of web-based public information.
作者： Kathleen McPhaul, Young Yun, Donald K. Milton, Cynthia Baur
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述重点关注紫外线 C (UVC) 光预防空气传播感染的作用,检查其对抗流感、麻疹 和结核病等病原体的历史有效性。核心问题是,关于 UVC 技术的基于网络的公共信息的可用性、清晰度和可操作性是否影响了 COVID-19 大流行期间的政策采用和使用。
证据基础与方法
这是对基于网络的公共信息的基线分析。研究人员通过 Google 搜索 UVC 信息,参考公共卫生和监管机构的网页,并使用 CDC 的清晰沟通指数 (CCI) 对其进行评估,以评估清晰度和可操作性。首席研究员和一名博士生对最终样本进行了评分。
共识与结论
公共卫生和监管机构在 UVC 信息的清晰度、可操作性和集成方面还有很大的改进空间。该分析发现,有关用于预防空气传播感染的 UVC 的面向公众的信息存在政策沟通差距,这可能限制了其在 COVID-19 大流行期间的采用。
争议与缺口
缺乏明确的公共政策和对 UVC 的安全担忧可能会阻碍明确的公共卫生信息传递。中立、权威的组织需要提供更加综合、清晰和可操作的指导,以提高公众对 UVC 技术的理解和适当使用。
科研与实践启示
在可靠的公共卫生和监管网站上轻松搜索 UVC 消息可以为非专业受众翻译同行评审的文献并提高公众的理解。权威组织提供的更综合的指导可能会支持知情讨论和适当使用 UVC 作为保护室内空间人员的潜在救生方法。

Ultraviolet-C light for prevention of airborne infection: A baseline analysis of web-based public information.

Authors: Kathleen McPhaul, Young Yun, Donald K. Milton, Cynthia Baur
Original abstract (English)
Ultraviolet-C (UVC) technology to mitigate airborne infections was first demonstrated in laboratory and epidemiologic studies of influenza and measles in the 1930s, with effectiveness against tuberculosis demonstrated in the 1950s and again in the early 2000s. Yet, UVC was underutilized during the SARS CoV-2 pandemic (COVID-19). Availability, clarity, and actionability of information and messaging on UVC technology may have played a role in limiting policy discussions and uptake of UVC during the COVID-19 pandemic. The purpose of this analysis was to systematically assess the availability, clarity, and actionability of web-based information about UVC for preventing airborne respiratory infections. Student research assistants conducted internet searches using the Google Search engine after receiving a brief orientation to germicidal ultraviolet (GUV) air disinfection. They referred webpages from public health and regulatory organizations whose missions were to regulate UVC or provide recommendations for UVC to prevent transmission of airborne infectious pathogens to the principal investigator (PI). The PI determined whether the webpages met the inclusion criteria. The PI and a doctoral student with experience and training scored the final sample using the CDC's Clear Communication Index (CCI) which assessed public health information on clarity and actionability across four domains. Searches using the Google Search engine for information targeted to the general public yielded mostly commercial websites of UVC vendors, peer-reviewed papers, press releases on UVC research, and public health and regulatory agencies such as the Food and Drug Administration (FDA), the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). We found that the public health and regulatory agencies have much room for improvement in the clarity, actionability, and integration with each other of their messaging and information. The lack of public policy and concerns about safety of UVC may be inhibiting clear and definitive public health messaging. Easily searchable UVC messaging on credible public health and regulatory websites has the potential to translate the peer-reviewed literature for lay audiences and improve public understanding of UVC for reducing airborne infections. In conclusion, this baseline analysis suggests a policy-communication gap in public-facing information about UVC for airborne infection prevention. More integrated, clear, and actionable guidance from neutral, authoritative organizations may support public understanding, informed discussion, and appropriate use of this potentially life-saving approach to protecting people in indoor spaces.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on Ultraviolet-C (UVC) light for preventing airborne infections, examining its historical effectiveness against pathogens like influenza, measles, and tuberculosis. The central question is whether the availability, clarity, and actionability of web-based public information on UVC technology influenced its policy adoption and usage during the COVID-19 pandemic.
Evidence base and method
This is a baseline analysis of web-based public information. Researchers conducted Google searches for UVC information, referred webpages from public health and regulatory agencies, and assessed them using the CDC's Clear Communication Index (CCI) to evaluate clarity and actionability. The principal investigator and a doctoral student scored the final sample.
Consensus and conclusions
Public health and regulatory agencies have significant room for improvement in the clarity, actionability, and integration of their UVC messaging. The analysis identifies a policy-communication gap in public-facing information about UVC for airborne infection prevention, which may have limited its uptake during the COVID-19 pandemic.
Controversies and gaps
The lack of clear public policy and safety concerns about UVC may be inhibiting definitive public health messaging. There is a need for more integrated, clear, and actionable guidance from neutral, authoritative organizations to improve public understanding and appropriate use of UVC technology.
Research and practice implications
Easily searchable UVC messaging on credible public health and regulatory websites could translate peer-reviewed literature for lay audiences and improve public understanding. More integrated guidance from authoritative organizations may support informed discussion and appropriate use of UVC as a potentially life-saving approach for protecting people in indoor spaces.
DOI · PubMed · 来源DOI · PubMed · 来源
Journal: medRxiv  |  Canonical publication date: 2026-07-27  |  DOI: 10.64898/2026.07.24.26358371  |  Date basis: first_publication_date  |  Publication status: Current window  |  Citation: 2026

印度南部农村和部落环境的儿童免疫覆盖率以及评估 Covid19 大流行的影响

Childhood immunisation coverage in rural and a tribal settings in southern India and assessing effect of the Covid19 pandemic
作者： Reshma Raju, Rohan Michael Ramesh, Syam Prasad, Kulandaipalayam Natarajan Sindhu, Saravanakumar Puthupalayam Kaliappan, Kumudha Aruldas, Jayaprakash Muliyil, Mahesh Moorthy, Rajeev Zachariah Kompithra, Sitara Swarna Rao Ajjampur
摘要中文翻译摘要 目的 在新冠病毒大流行期间,全球儿童免疫接种覆盖率下降且出现延误。这项研究旨在查明印度南部儿童常规免疫接种方面的差距以及该流行病对人口普查的农村和部落人口的影响。方法 这项研究于 2021 年 8 月至 9 月期间在泰米尔纳德邦一个难以到达的部落街区(贾瓦杜山)和农村街区(蒂米里)进行。完全免疫覆盖率(FIC)、适龄覆盖率和疫苗接种延迟的比例是在大流行前(2020年3月之前)和大流行(封锁)期间计算的。结果 在这些人群中接受调查的 2746 名儿童(年龄 <30 月龄,有免疫卡)中,12-23 月龄儿童的总体 FIC 在贾瓦杜为 50.8%(95%CI:46.6-55.0),在贾瓦杜为 83.7%;(81.1-86.2)在蒂米里。在贾瓦杜,口服脊髓灰质炎疫苗3 (OPV3) 的覆盖率从大流行前的 78.3% (73.4-82.6) 下降到大流行期间的 70.0% (66.6-73.2);灭活脊髓灰质炎疫苗2(IPV2)从76.9%(72.1-81.2)降至69.1%(65.5-72.4);五价3从 78.0% (73.1-82.4) 降至 70.2% (66.8-73.5);和麻疹含有疫苗1(MCV1)从74.6%(67.6-80.8)到56.7%(53.3-60.1)。在 Timiri,仅 MCV1 覆盖率从 92.9% (88.7-95.9) 下降至 85.4% (83.3-87.3)。与未接受 MCV1 相关的因素包括 疫苗接种 的预产期在大流行封锁期间下降、出生顺序较高、父母年轻且受教育程度较低以及居住在部落街区。结论 本研究强调了地理偏远部落地区与更容易到达的农村地区常规儿童免疫 疫苗 覆盖率和大流行影响的差异。具有复原力的卫生系统对于确保 疫苗接种 覆盖范围收益的可持续性至关重要,特别是在偏远地区,并且应该成为大流行病防范的一部分。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本次审查调查了印度南部农村和部落环境中的儿童免疫覆盖率,特别评估了 Covid-19 大流行对常规 疫苗接种 服务的影响。
证据基础与方法
2021 年 8 月至 9 月期间,在泰米尔纳德邦的贾瓦杜山(部落)和蒂米里(农村)两个街区进行了一项横断面研究,涉及 2746 名 30 个月以下且拥有免疫卡的儿童。
共识与结论
与农村地区 (83.7%) 相比,部落地区的全面免疫覆盖率 (FIC) 显着较低 (50.8%)。疫情导致部落地区多种疫苗的覆盖率下降,MCV1从74.6%下降至56.7%,而农村地区仅MCV1下降(92.9%至85.4%)。
争议与缺口
该研究强调了部落和农村人口之间的差异,但没有解决 疫苗接种 延误的长期后果,也没有探索偏远地区的潜在解决方案,除了建议有弹性的卫生系统之外。
科研与实践启示
具有复原力的卫生系统对于维持 疫苗接种 覆盖范围的扩大至关重要,特别是在偏远地区,并且应将其纳入大流行病防备战略中。

Childhood immunisation coverage in rural and a tribal settings in southern India and assessing effect of the Covid19 pandemic

Authors: Reshma Raju, Rohan Michael Ramesh, Syam Prasad, Kulandaipalayam Natarajan Sindhu, Saravanakumar Puthupalayam Kaliappan, Kumudha Aruldas, Jayaprakash Muliyil, Mahesh Moorthy, Rajeev Zachariah Kompithra, Sitara Swarna Rao Ajjampur
Original abstract (English)
ABSTRACT Purpose Childhood immunisation witnessed reduced coverage and delays globally during the Covid19 pandemic. This study aimed to identify gaps in routine childhood immunisation in southern India and the effect of the pandemic in censused rural and tribal populations. Methods This study was conducted between August and September 2021 in a hard-to-reach tribal block (Jawadhu Hills) and rural (Timiri) block in Tamil Nadu. The proportion of full immunisation coverage (FIC), age-appropriate coverage and vaccination delays were calculated in the pre-pandemic (prior to March 2020) and pandemic (lockdown) period. Results Among 2746 children (aged <30 months, with immunisation cards available) surveyed in these populations, overall FIC among children between 12-23 months of age was 50.8% (95%CI: 46.6-55.0) in Jawadhu and 83.7%; (81.1-86.2) in Timiri. In Jawadhu, coverage of oral polio vaccine3 (OPV3) decreased from 78.3% (73.4-82.6) pre-pandemic to 70.0% (66.6-73.2) during the pandemic; inactivated polio vaccine2 (IPV2) from 76.9% (72.1-81.2) to 69.1% (65.5-72.4); Pentavalent3 from 78.0% (73.1-82.4) to 70.2% (66.8-73.5); and Measles containing vaccine1 (MCV1) from 74.6% (67.6-80.8) to 56.7% (53.3-60.1). In Timiri, only MCV1 coverage decreased from 92.9% (88.7-95.9) to 85.4% (83.3-87.3). Factors associated with not receiving MCV1 included due date of vaccination falling during the pandemic lock downs, higher birth order, younger and less educated parents, and residing in the tribal block. Conclusion This study highlighted differences in routine childhood immunisation vaccine coverage and impact of the pandemic in a geographically remote tribal block in contrast to a more accessible, rural block. Resilient health systems are essential to ensure sustainability in gains in vaccination coverage, especially in remote areas, and should be part of pandemic preparedness.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
This review examines childhood immunisation coverage in rural and tribal settings in southern India, specifically assessing the impact of the Covid-19 pandemic on routine vaccination services.
Evidence base and method
A cross-sectional study conducted between August and September 2021 in two blocks: Jawadhu Hills (tribal) and Timiri (rural) in Tamil Nadu, involving 2746 children under 30 months with available immunisation cards.
Consensus and conclusions
Full immunisation coverage (FIC) was significantly lower in the tribal block (50.8%) compared to the rural block (83.7%). The pandemic led to decreased coverage for multiple vaccines in the tribal area, with MCV1 dropping from 74.6% to 56.7%, while only MCV1 decreased in the rural area (92.9% to 85.4%).
Controversies and gaps
The study highlights disparities between tribal and rural populations but does not address long-term consequences of vaccination delays or explore potential solutions for remote areas beyond suggesting resilient health systems.
Research and practice implications
Resilient health systems are essential to maintain vaccination coverage gains, particularly in remote areas, and should be integrated into pandemic preparedness strategies.

麻疹 变得如此普遍,以至于可能很快就会需要 治疗 - Wired.com

Measles Is Becoming So Common That Treatments May Soon Be Needed - wired.com
新闻简报美国正在经历数十年来最高的 麻疹 病例,到 2026 年 7 月,南卡罗来纳州和犹他州的 麻疹 病例数量已超过 2,289 例,超过 2024 年全年总数。疫苗接种 比率下降,低于社区保护所需的 95%,重新点燃了人们对 治疗 的兴趣。幼儿面临严重的风险,包括肺炎、耳聋和脑损伤。目前,医生依赖支持性护理,但 Invivyd 等生物技术公司正在开发类似于用于治疗 COVID-19 的单克隆抗体。这些疗法可以为高危人群提供短期保护,尽管人体试验和监管批准还需要数年时间。随着 麻疹 变得越来越普遍,这种现象的复苏凸显了准备工作中的一个关键差距。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 27 日。活动时间范围包括 2026 个 暴发,截至 7 月,案件数量已超过 2024 个。
地点与对象
美国,南卡罗来纳州和犹他州有 暴发。受影响人群包括幼儿、免疫功能低下的个体和 12 个月以下的婴儿。
事件
确认 麻疹 在美国卷土重来,到 2026 年 7 月,病例数超过 2,289 例(2024 年总数),达到 35 年来的最高水平。由于 疫苗接种 发病率下降,研究人员正在开发 治疗,包括单克隆抗体。
规模、影响与风险

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles Is Becoming So Common That Treatments May Soon Be Needed - wired.com

News Brief
The US is experiencing its highest measles cases in decades, with outbreaks in South Carolina and Utah driving numbers past 2,289 by July 2026—surpassing the total for all of 2024. Declining vaccination rates, falling below the 95% needed for community protection, have reignited interest in treatments. Young children face severe risks, including pneumonia, deafness, and brain damage. Currently, doctors rely on supportive care, but biotech firms like Invivyd are developing monoclonal antibodies similar to those used for COVID-19. These therapies could provide short-term protection for high-risk individuals, though human trials and regulatory approval remain years away. The resurgence highlights a critical gap in preparedness as measles becomes more common.

Skip to main content SECURITY POLITICS THE BIG STORY BUSINESS SCIENCE CULTURE REVIEWS Newsletters Security Politics The Big Story Business Science Culture Reviews More The Big InterviewMagazineEventsWIRED InsiderWIRED Consulting Newsletters Podcasts Video Livestreams Merch Search Sign In Sign In Emily Mullin Science Jul 27, 2026 5:30 AM Measles Is Becoming So Common That Treatments May Soon Be Needed As the US sees its highest number of measles cases in decades and vaccination rates fall, researchers are developing drugs to help those who contract the virus or who are particularly vulnerable. Photo-illustration: WIRED Staff; Getty Images 8Save this story 8Save this story Up until recently, measles vaccination has been so effective that there’s been little medical or financial incentive to develop treatments for the disease. But as measles cases hit a 35-year high and vaccination rates continue to decline in the US, a handful of biotech companies and academic groups have started working on measles treatments for those infected with the virus as well as vulnerable individuals needing short-term protection. It will likely be years, however, before these countermeasures are available, and it’s even more uncertain if those who refuse vaccines would take such treatments. Fueled by large outbreaks in South Carolina and Utah, the US had already surpassed last year’s 2,289 measles cases, and it’s only July. For comparison, there were just 285 cases in all of 2024. Featured Video Because measles is so contagious, at least 95 percent of people in a community need to receive both doses of the measles vaccine to protect immunocompromised individuals and infants under 12 months old who are too young to be vaccinated. But measles vaccination rates have dipped well below that in many places across the country. Young children are the most susceptible to severe complications from measles. The virus can cause pneumonia by damaging the respiratory tract lining directly and by suppressing the immune system, leaving them susceptible to bacterial infections. It can also cause deafness and potentially fatal brain damage. Without a measles-specific treatment, doctors rely on supportive care such as preventing dehydration, managing breathing difficulties, and administering steroids for inflammation. Vitamin A is sometimes given to patients because the virus can deplete levels of it in the body, but the supplement has no effect on the virus itself. “What we’re seeing now as we get a resurgence of measles in communities is a horrible lack of options when somebody gets exposed,” says Michael Mina, an epidemiologist and chief medical officer of Invivyd, a Connecticut-based biotech company. Invivyd is developing a type of therapy for measles known as a monoclonal antibody, which mimics natural antibodies the immune system makes to fight off disease. Given via IV or injection, monoclonal antibodies were widely used to treat and prevent Covid-19, especially in immunocompromised individuals who didn’t mount a strong immune response to the vaccines. Their protective effects can last up to a few months. Invivyd, which developed a monoclonal antibody for Covid, has been getting inquiries from health care providers about when a similar therapy will be available for measles. In the event of a local measles outbreak, a monoclonal antibody could provide short-term protection to those at high risk of contracting the virus. Invivyd has not yet tested its measles antibody in humans, but Mina says the company is compiling the data needed for the Food and Drug Administration to sign off on clinical trials. At the La Jolla Institute for Immunology, president and CEO Erica Ollmann Saphire has been trying to understand the human antibody response to measles. In May, Saphire and her team published a study describing human antibodies capable of blocking infection by binding to key sites on the measles virus. An infusion of these antibodies resulted in a 500-fold lower viral load in rodents. A key step toward getting these treatments into human trials will be manufacturing monoclonal antibodies at scale, which is a costly and complex process. Clinical trials cost millions of dollars to run, and pharmaceutical partners are usually needed to fund them. But measles treatments could be a hard sell for pharma companies. Drugs for infectious diseases aren’t usually big money-makers because of the sporadic nature of outbreaks and short treatment courses. Most Popular Digital Culture Don’t Get Too Attached to Jimothy By Miles Klee Culture Boomers Can’t Stop Gifting Their Grandkids AI-Generated Slop Books By Miles Klee Space NASA’s New 3D Model Shows the Earth Is a Lumpy Mess By Javier Carbajal Cyberattacks and Hacks OpenAI’s Hacking Debacle Comes Down to Human Error By Lily Hay Newman “I think this is a real opportunity to solve a public health problem,” Saphire says. “A tool by which you could prevent or treat disease is still cheaper than managing an outbreak or managing a case of fatal encephalitis in a child.” Some disease models predict that measles could become endemic in the US in the next 25 years, even at current state-level vaccination rates. Monoclonal antibodies could also be costly for patients and insurers. In early 2022, the US government spent $855 million for 1.7 million doses of Evusheld, a monoclonal antibody treatment authorized for Covid. The medication itself was free to patients, but some people were hit with hundreds of dollars in out-of-pocket costs related to the administration of the drug. The monoclonal antibody combination that became Evusheld was created in just 25 days by scientists at Vanderbilt University, which have also discovered potent antibodies for measles. The university has partnered with small biotech firm Saravir Biopharma to continue development. James Crowe, an immunologist and director of the Vanderbilt Center for Antibody Therapeutics who led the Covid work, says the strategy is to use a combination of two monoclonal antibodies to drastically reduce the chances that the measles virus could mutate to evade it. His group has been systematically making human antibodies against viruses that are most likely to cause future pandemics and happened to be in the middle of the measles work last year when US measles cases intensified. "The antibodies that we have isolated are so potent, they strongly inhibit the virus. We feel for sure they could be formulated as a single, long acting shot,” Crowe says. Once these monoclonal antibodies are ready for human testing, regulators will need to figure out how to evaluate them in clinical trials. New drugs are typically tested in adults first to make sure they’re safe for children. But complications from measles are more likely to hospitalize children, particularly those who are unvaccinated. Antivirals could also be used as a strategy to treat measles, although they tend to be short-lived and must often be given very soon after a person is infected or starts to develop symptoms to be effective. Researchers at Georgia State University identified an oral antiviral drug and tested it in rats, ferrets, and dogs infected with related viruses in the orthoparamyxovirus subfamily since these animals don’t get human measles. The drug lowered viral load and all treated animals survived. It’s not yet been tested in people. A challenge in antiviral development is that drugs toxic enough to kill viruses often have unpleasant side effects for people. The big question, though, is if people will use the treatments being developed or allow their children to take them. While anti-vaccine sentiment is driving the current outbreaks, Mina of Invivyd hopes people who have not vaccinated their children or gotten the vaccine themselves will be open to getting a monoclonal antibody because “this is a molecule that our bodies create naturally.” But during the Utah measles outbreak, some parents have refused to let doctors give their children immune globulin, a broad mix of antibodies derived from donated plasma. And anti-science sentiment has become more mainstream with Robert F. Kennedy Jr. leading the Department of Health and Human Services, where he has cast doubt on vaccines, treatments for depression, and other established medical treatments. That doesn’t mean researching antibodies and other treatments for measles isn’t worth the effort, though, particularly given the potential to help populations who are unable to be vaccinated. Crowe says it’s important that work on measles treatments gets funded as vaccination rates continue to decline. “I think there’s going to be hundreds of thousands of cases,” he says. “People are going to die. There’s some urgency to finishing this work.” Comments (8) Back to top Join the discussion Comments (8) Back to top Join the WIRED community to add comments. 对麻疹风险的“失忆”推动美国感染人数增长 - DW.com

'Amnesia' about measles risk pushes growth in US infections - DW.com
新闻简报美国正在经历一场严重的 麻疹 麻疹 疫情,2026 年 1 月至 7 月中旬的病例数已超过 2025 年全年的总数。公共卫生专家将这一激增归因于 麻疹 发病率下降以及他们所谓的对 麻疹 危险的“失忆症”,因为自 2000 年消除该疾病以来,该疾病已基本消失在日常生活中。到2026年,报告了2,321例病例,创下35年来的新高。这种具有高度传染性的病毒现在正在美国境内传播,而不仅仅限于输入病例,威胁到该国的 麻疹 消除状态。卫生官员警告说,许多人低估了 麻疹 的严重性,它可能导致肺炎、脑炎和死亡,并发症有时会在感染数年后出现。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

'Amnesia' about measles risk pushes growth in US infections - DW.com

News Brief
The United States is experiencing a significant measles outbreak, with cases from January to mid-July 2026 already exceeding the total for all of 2025. Public health experts attribute this surge to declining vaccination rates and what they term 'amnesia' about measles' dangers, as the disease has been largely absent from everyday life since its elimination in 2000. By July 2026, 2,321 cases had been reported, marking a 35-year high. The highly contagious virus is now spreading within the US rather than being limited to imported cases, threatening the country's measles-elimination status. Health officials warn that many people underestimate the severity of measles, which can cause pneumonia, encephalitis, and death, with complications sometimes appearing years after infection.
Original source text (English)
You need to enable JavaScript to run this app. Skip to content Skip to main menu Skip to more DW sites Latest videos Latest audio Regions Africa Asia Europe Latin America Middle East North America Germany Topics Climate Equality Health Human Rights Migration Technology Categories Business Science Environment Culture Sports In focus BerlinIranWildfires Latest audioLatest videos Live TV Advertisement ScienceUnited States of America 'Amnesia' about measles risk pushes growth in US infections Richard Connor 07/27/2026July 27, 2026 The latest US data show measles cases from January to mid-July 2026 totaled more than all cases in 2025. Experts blame a fall in vaccination and warn that people have forgotten measles can be life-threatening. https://p.dw.com/p/5HiUO Copy link The measles vaccination is strongly recommended, but some parents are not heeding the callImage: Erin Hooley/Chicago Tribune/TNS/Sipa USA/picture alliance Advertisement Behind the familiar symptoms of fever and spots is an extremely contagious virus that can cause lasting harm or death. That's the reality of measles. But many people in the US appear to have forgotten the seriousness of a measles infection, with outbreaks mainly in under-vaccinated communities. In fact, infections have hit a 35-year high as declining vaccination rates have created growing pockets of susceptible people. Experts warn that the fading memories of the once-common disease have left too many people unaware of its dangers. The number of measles cases in the United States up to mid-July 2026 surpassed the total number of cases in 2025. By the week of July 13-19, 2026, the number of reported measles cases was 2,321. By comparison, the total number of US measles cases in 2025 was 2,286, according to the US Measles Tracker run by the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health. Advertisement What does the rise in measles cases mean? Johns Hopkins epidemiologist William Moss expects the surge in measles cases to result in the US losing the measles-elimination status it has held since 2000. "The significance of reaching this milestone is that the US has not been able to stop measles virus transmission or outbreaks as it has in the past," Moss told DW. Moss said the virus was now spreading "from one susceptible community to another within the US," rather than being limited to infections brought in from abroad. Measles virus is contagious before spots appear Measles is the most contagious virus on Earth, spreading far more easily than the common cold. Nine out of 10 non-immune people exposed to the virus will become infected. Measles spreads through virus-containing droplets released from an infected person's nose, throat and lungs when they breathe, talk or cough. The smallest particles can remain suspended in the air long after the person has left the room. Is the US experiencing a vaccine crisis? To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video 05:18 "You just have to be in somebody's airspace within two hours of them being there," said Paul Offit, director of the Vaccine Education Center at the Children's Hospital of Philadelphia. Crucially, people can transmit measles before realizing they are infected. Patients typically become contagious around four days before the characteristic spots or rash appear. The combination of airborne particles, extreme contagion and transmission before obvious symptoms allows the virus to exploit even relatively small gaps in vaccination. Measles complications can emerge years later In the US, about one in five people with measles are hospitalized. One in 20 infected children develops pneumonia, while around one in 1,000 develops encephalitis, a form of brain inflammation that can cause permanent brain damage. Between one and three of every 1,000 infected children die from respiratory or neurological complications. "We had two unvaccinated children — a healthy little six-year-old, a healthy little eight-year-old — die of measles this past year," Offit told DW. Both died with pneumonia. "It's like dying of suffocation," he said. "It's a terrible way to die." Among those who survive, the harm can persist long after the fever and spots disappear. Measles can suppress the immune system for years, erasing some of the protection it had previously developed against other viruses and bacteria. Another delayed complication is subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, a rare but fatal degenerative brain disease that can appear up to 10 years after the original infection. Measles spots spread downward across the body in an easy-to-recognize mannerImage: Alejandro Ernesto/dpa/picture alliance "Children get infected, they get better, they're fine," Offit said. "And then five years later, seven years later, they come in with a deterioration in handwriting skills, deterioration in personality and then they descend into a vegetative state and die." Has the danger of measles been forgotten? Vaccination pushed measles out of everyday life in the US. But that success has also weakened public understanding of why vaccination is necessary. "We've not just largely eliminated measles from our country; we've eliminated the memory of measles," Offit said. "The most serious complications of measles are relatively rare and only become apparent when there is a large number of cases," said Moss. "Unfortunately, the increasing number of measles cases in the US will once again teach us that this is not always a harmless childhood infection." Katelyn Jetelina, an epidemiologist who publishes a Substack newsletter called "Your Local Epidemiologist," said falling vaccination rates reflected a "general amnesia about these diseases," falsehoods on social media and a wider loss of trust in institutions. "The outbreaks remain among under-vaccinated communities," Jetelina said. "There are unvaccinated pockets all around the nation. All it takes is a spark for this disease to spread like wildfire in those pockets." 安克雷奇废水中发现 麻疹病毒 的证据 - 阿拉斯加公共媒体

Evidence of measles virus found in Anchorage wastewater - Alaska Public Media
新闻简报阿拉斯加公共媒体报道了 2026 年 7 月 7 日至 14 日安克雷奇废水中存在 麻疹病毒 的证据,表明该地区至少有一名感染者。州流行病学家 Joe McLaughlin 强调了 传播 来源的不确定性,并敦促临床医生测试和隔离疑似病例。该检测来自使用高灵敏度废水分析的国家试点计划,而州测试仍在以较低的灵敏度进行。自 2000 年以来,美国的 麻疹 活动达到历史最高水平,幼儿和免疫功能低下的人最容易受到影响。官员们正在调查病例是否来自旅行者或居民,并计划在八月初之前发布更多数据。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 29 日;活动日期:2026年7月7日、8日、9日和14日。
地点与对象
阿拉斯加州安克雷奇;安克雷奇污水流域集水区;阿拉斯加的其他地区没有纳入试点计划。
事件
在安克雷奇废水中发现 麻疹病毒 的证据;经国家流行病学家证实;正在调查中;阿拉斯加公共媒体报道。
规模、影响与风险
至少一人,可能多人被感染;没有指定具体情况;传播 的风险未知;自 2000 年以来,麻疹 在美国的活动达到历史最高水平;最危险的是:幼儿和免疫功能低下的人。
应对、状态与不确定性
分析废水中疾病 RNA 的国家试点计划;状态测试不太敏感但更快;建议临床医生对疑似病例进行检测和隔离;预计八月初会有更多数据;未知病例是来自旅行者还是居民;检测到野生型病毒,并非源自 疫苗。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Evidence of measles virus found in Anchorage wastewater - Alaska Public Media

News Brief
Alaska Public Media reports evidence of measles virus in Anchorage wastewater from July 7-14, 2026, indicating at least one infected person in the area. State epidemiologist Joe McLaughlin emphasized uncertainty about transmission sources and urged clinicians to test and isolate suspected cases. The detection comes from a national pilot program using highly sensitive wastewater analysis, while state testing continues with lower sensitivity. Measles activity in the US is at an all-time high since 2000, with young children and immunocompromised individuals most vulnerable. Officials are investigating whether cases stem from travelers or residents and plan to release more data by early August.

Trouble at Sea In My Family LISTEN KSKA Schedule Podcasts All Radio Programs Addressing Alaskans Alaska Economic Report Alaska Morning News Alaska News Nightly Hometown, Alaska Line One Outdoor Explorer State of Art Talk of Alaska Traveling Music KSKA Schedule Podcasts All Radio Programs Addressing Alaskans Alaska Economic Report Alaska Morning News Alaska News Nightly Hometown, Alaska Line One Outdoor Explorer State of Art Talk of Alaska Traveling Music NEWS Latest News Alaska Desk Alaska Survival Kit Anchorage Economy Education Environment Health National Outdoors Politics Public Safety The Weekender Subscribe: Daily Digest Latest News Alaska Desk Alaska Survival Kit Anchorage Economy Education Environment Health National Outdoors Politics Public Safety The Weekender Subscribe: Daily Digest LEARN Education Events AKPM Programs Resources for Parents and Educators Molly of Denali Watch PBS KIDS Workforce Development Education Events AKPM Programs Resources for Parents and Educators Molly of Denali Watch PBS KIDS Workforce Development SUPPORT Ways to Give Fund the Gap Campaign Benefits of Membership Corporate Sponsorship AKPM Merchandise Ways to Give Fund the Gap Campaign Benefits of Membership Corporate Sponsorship AKPM Merchandise ABOUT AKPM App Careers Community Contact E-Newsletters Events How Alaska Public Media Is Funded People Public Documents Public Meetings Station News AKPM App Careers Community Contact E-Newsletters Events How Alaska Public Media Is Funded People Public Documents Public Meetings Station News BROADCAST STATUS DONOR PORTAL AK PASSPORT Health Evidence of measles virus found in Anchorage wastewater Alaska Public Media | By Rachel Cassandra Published July 29, 2026 at 3:07 PM AKDT Twitter LinkedIn Email Bluesky Courtesy of Alaska Environmental Health Laboratory. Alaska wastewater testing samples from Anchorage, Fairbanks, Eagle River and Bethel as they get prepped for processing by the state in July, 2026. Evidence of the measles virus was found in Anchorage wastewater in July, according to state epidemiologists. The data is from a national pilot program that analyzes wastewater for disease and looks for ribonucleic acid, or RNA, of certain pathogens. Joe McLaughlin, head epidemiologist for the state, said that based on those findings, "We've got at least one, maybe several people in the Anchorage sewershed catchment area that had measles around these dates, and we don't know who they were, where they were, or whether transmission was occurring." Wastewater tested positive for evidence of measles on July 7, 8, 9 and 14, but the state doesn't have comparable data yet for dates beyond mid-July. Further data is likely to come in by early August, he said. McLaughlin said officials don't know yet whether the test results are due to a traveler passing through or an Anchorage resident, but clinicians should be vigilant and on the lookout for measles. "Anybody who presents to the urgent care, the emergency department, or in for a routine clinic visit, seeing a child with an acute viral illness that's compatible with measles, they should go ahead and test them and put them in isolation if they think that they do have measles," McLaughlin said. Clayton Weingartner, who heads the wastewater analysis program for the state, said the data is thanks to a new pilot program funded by a national nonprofit organization, which uses a highly sensitive process to analyze Anchorage's wastewater. No other parts of Alaska are included in the pilot program, he said. Alaska analyzes its own wastewater too, through the Department of Environmental Conservation, a lab at the University of Alaska Anchorage and in Bethel through the Yukon-Kuskokwim Health Corporation's Office of Environmental Health. Although state testing is less sensitive, if there are more cases of measles, Weingartner said it would likely show up in the state data too. That testing is analyzed more quickly than the pilot program and can be tracked online. He said the state has detected trace amounts of measles before. "For certain pathogens like measles, which are of such high consequence, we're really working on increasing that (testing) sensitivity," he said and that the state may start notifying the public moving forward if there are trace detections. Weingartner pointed out that a person cannot get measles from wastewater, that it's a respiratory disease. And he said that while people who get the measles vaccine do shed a form of the viral RNA, the testing can differentiate what he called the "wild type" of virus versus the vaccine-derived type. "It's the wild type that we are detecting," he said. The best way to prevent measles transmission is through vaccination, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Typically, children get vaccinated before kindergarten. Symptoms for measles include a rash, high fever, a cough and congestion, tiny white spots in the mouth and red, watery eyes. If you or a family member has symptoms, McLaughlin said it's best to call a clinic before getting tested so they can prevent other patients from being exposed. Measles activity in the United States is at an all-time high since the disease was declared eliminated in the country in 2000. So, McLaughlin said it's really important for Alaskans to take precautions. "Measles could be imported from an Alaskan who's returning to Alaska from the Lower 48, or from abroad, or a tourist," he said. Measles is a very unpleasant disease to get, McLaughlin said, and long-term effects of the illness can include lifelong neurological consequences and occasionally, death. Those most at risk of hospitalization are young children and immunocompromised people. Most elders over age 60, McLaughlin said, were exposed during childhood or later vaccinated, so have immunity to the virus. Immunity through vaccination is lifelong. Health Twitter LinkedIn Email Bluesky Rachel Cassandra Rachel Cassandra covers health and wellness for Alaska Public Media. Reach her at rcassandra@alaskapublic.org. See stories by Rachel Cassandra

Response, status and uncertainty

Grand­parents for Vaccines advocate for vac­ci­nation as Ohio grapples with third Measles outbreak - Spectrum News

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Grand­parents for Vaccines advocate for vac­ci­nation as Ohio grapples with third Measles outbreak - Spectrum News

News Brief
Ohio has announced its third measles outbreak of 2026, with 15 cases reported so far this year. This follows 45 cases in 2025 and a national surge of 2,318 cases in 2026, already exceeding last year's total. The CDC reports that 93% of this year's cases occurred in unvaccinated individuals or those with unknown vaccine status. In response, the advocacy group Grandparents for Vaccines, including infectious disease specialists, is promoting vaccination. They emphasize the MMR vaccine's 97% effectiveness and warn about measles' severe complications, including death. The group's members, who remember pre-vaccination era fears, express concern about rising vaccine hesitancy.
Original source text (English)
CLEVELAND — The Ohio Department of Health (ODH) announced the state’s third measles outbreak of 2026 Thursday, as the nation grapples with surging infection rates. ODH has reported 15 measles cases in 2026. The state had 45 cases in 2025, seven cases in 2024 and one case in 2023. Across the country, the Centers for Disease Control (CDC) is reporting 2,318 cases this year, already surpassing the number of infections for the entire year of 2025. The CDC reports that 93% of the infections in 2026 were in unvaccinated people or individuals with an unknown vaccine status. Measles causes a host of flu-like symptoms including sore throat, cough and fever. It's most recognizable for its blotchy, red rash that spreads from the face down. While many of the people infected recover, the illness can lead to severe complications or even death. “The virus stays in the environment for hours. So if you bring a young child to a pediatrician's office and you're in the waiting room, people who enter that waiting room hours later can contract measles,” Dr. Judith Feinberg, an infectious disease specialist, said. Feinberg is a part of the advocacy group Grandparents for Vaccines, a group of grandparents dedicated to promoting vaccination. “If with a simple vaccination, you could prevent children from being hooked up to a ventilator to breathe or dying from, you know, a fatal brain infection, like to me, that's sort of a no brainer,” Feinberg said. “I have very vivid memories of what life was like before we started vaccinating children,” Mary Koslap-Petraco, another member of Grandparents for Vaccines, said. Koslap-Petraco’s sister was infected with measles when they were children. “I was terrified that she was going to die,” Koslap-Petraco said. “She looked awful. My mother wouldn't even let us in the room with her.” When vaccines became readily available, Koslap-Petraco remembers being thankful. “I used to say, 'wow, I live in a world where parents don't have to worry about losing their children to these horrible diseases,'” Koslap-Petraco said. “Now here I am a dinosaur, thinking to myself, 'oh my God, these poor parents are so afraid of vaccines. They don't know how bad these diseases are.' Am I going to be faced with looking at baby tombstones again?” The measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine provides 97% protection against measles for life, according to the CDC. Further vaccination guidelines are available here

Response, status and uncertainty
Grandparents for Vaccines, an advocacy group including infectious disease specialists, is promoting vaccination. The MMR vaccine provides 97% lifelong protection against measles. No specific response measures beyond vaccination advocacy are detailed in the evidence.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

W.Va. Confirms Measles Case In Barbour County - West Virginia Public Broadcasting

News Brief
West Virginia has confirmed its first measles case in over two years, reported in Barbour County by West Virginia Public Broadcasting. The single case marks a significant public health concern as measles is highly contagious and can lead to severe complications, especially in unvaccinated populations. Health officials have not yet released details on potential exposures, contact tracing efforts, or the vaccination status of the affected individual. This outbreak highlights the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent the spread of measles, which was declared eliminated in the U.S. in 2000 but has seen recent resurgences in areas with low vaccination coverage.
Original source text (English)

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

我是一名传染病医生,曾在 2015 年 麻疹 治疗过 麻疹。人们经常犯错的 7 件事 - TODAY.com

I’m an Infectious Disease Doctor Who Treated Measles in the 2015 Outbreak. 7 Things People Keep Getting Wrong - TODAY.com
新闻简报随着美国的 麻疹 病例达到 35 年来的新高,一位在 2015 年迪士尼乐园 麻疹 期间治疗患者的传染病专家揭穿了常见的误解。文章强调,由于 麻疹 病例自 2000 年消除以来非常罕见,许多临床医生从未见过 麻疹 病例,这可能会延迟 麻疹 病例。麻疹 具有高度传染性,会产生严重并发症,包括住院(每 5 名未接种疫苗的患者中就有 1 人)、肺炎(每 20 名儿童中就有 1 人)、脑炎(每 1,000 人中就有 1 人)和死亡(每 1,000 人中 1-3 人)。虽然 MMR 疫苗 的有效率为 97%,但可能会发生突破性感染,尽管症状较轻。作者强调了早期 诊断 和患者倡导的重要性,并指出该病毒可以在空气中存活长达两个小时。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 30 日。该文章引用了 2015 年的 麻疹 麻疹 和 2026 年 麻疹 感染量目前的 35 年来最高点。
地点与对象
2015 年 暴发 蔓延到六个州和加拿大。目前的 暴发 影响美国人口,重点关注未接种疫苗的个人和高危人群(5 岁以下儿童、20 岁以上成人、孕妇、免疫功能低下者和维生素 A 缺乏者)。
事件
在美国面临病例复苏之际,一位传染病专家揭穿了对 麻疹 的常见误解。本文基于作者在 2015 年迪士尼乐园 暴发 期间治疗患者的经验,并解决了当前的公共卫生问题。
规模、影响与风险
报告称,2026 年 麻疹 感染人数创 35 年来新高,病例总数超过上一年。五分之一的未接种疫苗的患者住院,每 20 名儿童中就有 1 名患上肺炎,每 1,000 名儿童中就有 1 名患有脑炎,每 1,000 名儿童中有 1 到 3 名死亡。该病毒具有高度传染性,能够在空气中存活长达两个小时。
应对、状态与不确定性
该文章强调了由于 麻疹 病例自 2000 年消除以来的罕见性,临床医生之间存在知识差距,这可能会延迟 麻疹 的消除。它强调早期发现和患者倡导的重要性。来源是 TODAY.com 的完整原始报告,提供了专家见解​​,但没有官方公共卫生应对细节。

I’m an Infectious Disease Doctor Who Treated Measles in the 2015 Outbreak. 7 Things People Keep Getting Wrong - TODAY.com

News Brief
As measles cases in the U.S. reach a 35-year high, an infectious disease specialist who treated patients during the 2015 Disneyland outbreak debunks common misconceptions. The article highlights that many clinicians have never seen a measles case due to its rarity since elimination in 2000, potentially delaying diagnosis. Measles is highly contagious, with severe complications including hospitalization (1 in 5 unvaccinated patients), pneumonia (1 in 20 children), encephalitis (1 in 1,000), and death (1-3 per 1,000). While the MMR vaccine is 97% effective, breakthrough infections can occur, though symptoms are milder. The author stresses the importance of early diagnosis and patient advocacy, noting the virus can survive in the air for up to two hours.
Original source text (English)

July 30, 2026, 8:16 PM UTC / Source: TODAY Shiv Sudhakar, MD Skip to table of contents 0 seconds of 2 minutes, 18 secondsVolume 0% Press shift question mark to access a list of keyboard shortcuts Keyboard ShortcutsEnabledDisabled Play/PauseSPACE Increase Volume↑ Decrease Volume↓ Seek Forward→ Seek Backward← Captions On/Offc Fullscreen/Exit Fullscreenf Mute/Unmutem Decrease Caption Size- Increase Caption Size+ or = Seek %0-9 Settings OffCCEnglish Font Color White Font Opacity 100% Font Size 100% Font Family Arial Character Edge None Background Color Black Background Opacity 50% Window Color Black Window Opacity 0% Reset WhiteBlackRedGreenBlueYellowMagentaCyan 100%75%50%25% 200%175%150%125%100%75%50% ArialCourierGeorgiaImpactLucida ConsoleTahomaTimes New RomanTrebuchet MSVerdana NoneRaisedDepressedUniformDrop Shadow WhiteBlackRedGreenBlueYellowMagentaCyan 100%75%50%25%0% WhiteBlackRedGreenBlueYellowMagentaCyan 100%75%50%25%0% Auto1080p (4871 kbps)720p (3513 kbps)540p (1768 kbps)360p (956 kbps)270p (499 kbps)270p (278 kbps) Live 00:00 02:18 02:18 Add TODAY to Google RFK Jr. Grilled on Measles Cases, End of Flu Vaccines for Troops 02:18 Create your free profile or sign in to save this article Add TODAY to Google When treating measles patients infected in the Disneyland outbreak that spread to six states and Canada between 2014 and 2015, I learned firsthand that even “the happiest place on Earth” is not immune to a preventable crisis. In fact, many of the urgent public health lessons I took away from this period feel just as relevant as the U.S. battles a 35-year high in measles infections. Last week, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported the number of measles cases in 2026 had surpassed 2025’s total, and the nation’s status of having eliminated the virus is at risk, NBC News reported. The chaos that followed the Disneyland outbreak was overwhelming, and the fear of a localized outbreak within the facility where I worked was terrifying. Measles is one of the most contagious viruses on Earth and can cause severe complications. Over a decade later, the short-lived “Disneyland effect” — which temporarily boosted public support for vaccinations — has faded from our collective memory, though it remains vivid in mine. As we face this resurgence, here’s what I want people to know about the measles virus. Your Doctor Probably Has Never Seen a Case of Measles I trained to be an infectious disease specialist. Yet, despite nine years of rigorous medical training, I never encountered a measles case until I was thrown into an outbreak years after graduating. And I’m not alone. With 1 million active physicians in the U.S., the odds of any individual clinician encountering the disease have been incredibly low over the past two decades. Following measles’ U.S. elimination in 2000, the country averaged 100-200 cases annually between 2001 and 2019 (outside of major outbreaks). Research shows this lack of firsthand exposure has created knowledge gaps that could delay making a measles diagnosis. And early diagnosis is important to prevent further spread and complications. Measles Is Not Easy to Diagnose Measles typically begins with non-specific, flu-like symptoms that are easily dismissed as a standard cold until the hallmark rash appears. In medical school, we are taught the “3 Cs” — cough, coryza (runny nose) and conjunctivitis (red eyes) — followed by a blotchy red rash that starts on the face and spreads downward. But when I encountered my first real patient, I almost missed the diagnosis. The only reason I tested for measles was my patient asked me to. Patients are sometimes their best advocate, so don’t be afraid to ask your doctor to order a test or think of diagnosis they may have missed. In its early stages, measles closely resembles many other illnesses. Even a routine allergic reaction to medication can cause a drug rash that looks virtually identical to measles to an untrained eye. Measles Is Not Just a Childhood Rash Measles is a highly contagious disease that can cause ear infections, diarrhea and severe complications in 30% of cases, including: Hospitalization: One in five unvaccinated U.S. patients end up hospitalized. Pneumonia: One out of 20 children develop a lung infection. Brain Swelling: One out of 1,000 children suffer from encephalitis (which can lead to deafness and intellectual disability). Death: The virus kills one to three out of every 1,000 children infected. (There are no documented cases of a healthy, vaccinated person dying of measles.) The risk is highest in children less than 5, adults older than 20, pregnant women, immunocompromised individuals and those with vitamin A deficiency. Measles can also cause complications after recovery. A rare and often fatal central nervous system disease called subacute sclerosing panencephalitis can strike seven to 10 years after the initial infection. Infants under 2 face the highest risk. You Can Still Get Measles If You Are Fully Vaccinated Two doses of the MMR vaccine provide lifelong protection in 97% of people, according to the CDC. Recommended Wellness WellnessAre the New Hoka Clifton Sneakers Worth It? An Editor Shares Her Thoughts Deals DealsScore up to 65% off Lululemon’s ‘We Made Too Much’ Section The remaining 3% are susceptible, usually because because their immune systems fail to produce enough antibodies, or intense, prolonged exposure overwhelms their defenses. (Think of living in a confined household where multiple unvaccinated individuals are actively shedding the virus.) But vaccinated individuals who experience breakthrough infections usually suffer a much milder symptoms and are much less likely to transmit the virus to others, research shows. Measles is one of the most contagious viral pathogens on Earth, capable of living in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves the room, so if you suspect you have measles, always call a health care facility before showing up so they can take proper isolation protocols. The Measles Vaccine Does Not Cause Person-to-Person Spread The measles vaccine uses a weakened version of the actual virus to safely mimic a natural infection, which results in long-lasting immunity. (It should not be used in immunocompromised people.) I’ve often been asked if a vaccinated person can shed the virus from the vaccine and endanger others, especially immunocompromised folks. Fortunately, the vaccine strain of measles cannot spread from person to person, per the CDC. Vitamin A Is a Supportive Treatment, Not a Cure-All I remember having to tell a measles patient’s mother during the 2015 outbreak that there is no approved antiviral cure, a humbling reminder of our clinical limits. Treatment is strictly supportive: acetaminophen or ibuprofen for fever and pain, aggressive hydration and lots of rest. Vitamin A can also be a supportive treatment, as the measles virus rapidly depletes levels of the nutrient in the blood. During the measles outbreak in Texas last year, the CDC said vitamin A should be given to children hospitalized with severe symptoms. At the same time, the agency also stressed that vitamin A does not prevent infection and is not a replacement for the vaccine. Giving vitamin A also necessitates oversight from a health care provider, since vitamin A toxicity comes with severe risks. The Measles Virus Is More Dangerous Than the Vaccine, Data Show Many patients asked me during the 2015 outbreak if the measles vaccine is safe, and I told them the dangers of catching measles are much greater than the side effects of the vaccine. There are no documented deaths in healthy people due to the measles vaccine, whereas up to three children out of every 1,000 infected with measles will die, according to the Infectious Diseases Society of America. The vaccine can very rarely cause fever-induced seizures — which are not associated with long-term health effects, per the CDC — and immune thrombocytopenic purpura, a rare condition that disrupts blood clotting. ITP is usually not life-threatening and occurs in about one out of 40,000 MMR vaccinations. A measles infection causes severe complications much more often: According to the CDC, about one in 20 children will develop pneumonia and one in 1,000 will develop dangerous swelling of the brain. Shiv Sudhakar, MD Dr. Shiv Sudhakar is a contributing writer for TODAY.com. After writing short stories in medical school, he learned humor really is the best medicine. Being a doctor inspires him to write stories that educate people to learn more about their body and health. As an infectious disease specialist, he has a particular interest in bugs and drugs. He also works in addiction medicine and is passionate about combating homelessness, decreasing substance abuse, improving mental health and elevating extraordinary stories from everyday people. 阿拉斯加最大城市的废水发出有关 麻疹 的警告

Wastewater in Alaska’s largest city signals warning about measles
新闻简报阿拉斯加州安克雷奇的卫生官员在 2026 年 7 月上旬和中旬收集的废水样本中检测到了 麻疹 遗传物质,表明该市最大的社区中至少有一名患有这种高度传染性疾病的人。这一检测起到了预警作用,促使官员敦促居民检查他们的 疫苗接种 状态。尽管截至周二尚未报告新病例,但由于阿拉斯加的 传播 率较低以及当前的旅游旺季,传播 的风险仍然较高。卫生官员正在继续处理废水 监测,并准备在出现更多病例时追踪潜在的接触者。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026年7月29日。活动日期:2026年7月上旬和中旬。
地点与对象
安克雷奇,阿拉斯加最大的城市,特别是 John M. Asplund 污水处理厂 治疗 所服务的地区。
事件
在废水样本中检测到 麻疹 遗传物质,表明社区中至少存在 1 名 麻疹 患者。该事件已得到卫生官员的证实。
规模、影响与风险
7月初和中旬至少有1例确诊病例。截至周二,没有新报告病例。该疾病具有高度传染性,在美国已造成 3 例 麻疹 死亡。由于 麻疹 发病率较低和旅游旺季,风险较高。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在等待最近的废水检测结果。公共卫生措施包括敦促 疫苗接种 并通知医疗服务提供者疑似病例。如果发现病例,则需要进行接触者追踪。测试过程复杂且昂贵,导致结果延迟。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wastewater in Alaska’s largest city signals warning about measles

News Brief
Health officials in Anchorage, Alaska, have detected measles genetic material in wastewater samples collected in early and mid-July 2026, indicating at least one person with the highly contagious disease was present in the city's largest community. The detection serves as an early warning, prompting officials to urge residents to check their vaccination status. While no new cases have been reported as of Tuesday, the risk of transmission remains elevated due to Alaska's low vaccination rates and the current peak tourist season. Health officials are continuing wastewater surveillance and preparing for potential contact tracing if additional cases emerge.
Original source text (English)
The gate to the John M. Asplund Wastewater Treatment Facility is seen on July 29, 2026. The plant serves most o the residents of Alaska's largest city. Wastewater samples taken in early and mid-July revealed genetic material from measles. (Photo by Yereth Rosen/Alaska Beacon) At least one person in Anchorage had measles in early and mid-July, creating a risk that the highly infectious disease could spread through the state's largest city, state health officials are warning. Samples taken from Anchorage's John M. Asplund Wastewater Treatment Facility revealed genetic material from the strain of measles that has caused outbreaks in the Lower 48. It means someone carrying the virus was in the part of Anchorage served by the state's biggest wastewater treatment plant, said Dr. Joe McLaughlin, Alaska's state epidemiologist. "It doesn't tell us where exactly they were in the sewershed, and it also doesn't tell us if there was any human-to-human transmission that's occurring," he said. But it does provide an early warning to health providers and the general public, McLaughlin said. SUBSCRIBE: GET THE MORNING HEADLINES DELIVERED TO YOUR INBOX. "It's important for people to take a look and see if they've been vaccinated, or if their children have been vaccinated," he said. If children have not been vaccinated, "go ahead and get them vaccinated, because we know that this virus has been present in the community. And it may still be present." The presence of measles RNA, the virus' single-strand genetic material, was detected in samples taken on July 7, 8, 9 and 14, health officials said. State and municipal health officials are still waiting for results from more recent wastewater testing, McLaughlin said. Samples from the Asplund plant are usually taken every Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday, said Clayton Weingartner, the Division of Public Health's wastewater surveillance coordinator. Performing the scan that detects measles genetic material is a complex process, he said. "it's an expensive test, and it takes longer to get the results," he said. That is why state officials are still waiting for results from last week's sampling, he said. For now, public health officials say, Anchorage residents and other Alaskans should be on the lookout for signs of measles . A measles-infected baby being treated in 2014 at a hospital in Manilla, the Philippines capital city, has a rash that is a hallmark symptom of the disease. The Philippines experiencing a large measles outbreak after Typhoon Haiyan, an especially powerful natural disaster. (Photo by Jim Goodson/U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) Symptoms, which usually arrive a week or two after infection, include a high fever, a runny nose, red eyes and a skin rash. The disease can be serious and even fatal, especially for young children. There were three measles deaths in the United States last year, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. As of Tuesday, there were no new reports of any patients with measles in Anchorage. The last Anchorage case was in October, affecting an adult who tested positive after traveling to the city from the Lower 48, according to municipal officials. There was also a child in Anchorage who was diagnosed with measles in May of 2025. Elsewhere in the state, there were four measles cases diagnosed last year, according to health officials. McLaughlin said there are reasons for Alaskans to be vigilant. There are more measles cases in the United States now than at any time in the past 35 yeas, he said. "And we're at peak tourist season right now, so there are people coming into Alaska from the Lower 48 and from many different countries, and they're bringing with them, some of them, different pathogens," he said. "And one of them can be measles. And we know that measles is the most contagious virus that we're aware of on the planet." Additionally, Alaskans are at elevated risk because the rate of measles vaccination is low compared to the rest of the nation. Medical providers should be notified if there are suspected measles infections, McLaughlin said. Patients should not be in clinic waiting rooms because the virus is so contagious, he said. Whenever a case is identified, health officials have to trace the places where that patient may have exposed others to the virus, such as daycare centers, stores and recreational sites. "All that kind of stuff takes a lot of work, and that's just for one case," he said. Wastewater sampling in Alaska screens for a variety of infectious diseases, including COVID-19, different varieties of influenza and Respiratory Syncytial Virus, or RSV, as well as measles. Numerous sites participate in the National Wastewater Surveillance System , or NWSS, that was set up by the CDC in 2020 as part of the national response to the COVID-19 pandemic. But the Asplund plant, which serves most of Anchorage's residents, is the only one in Alaska that participates in the most advanced testing program, a privately operated system called WastewaterSCAN , Weingartner said. "The WastewaterSCAN program has very limited testing slots," he said. The Alaska Department of Environmental Conservation, the University of Alaska Anchorage and the Yukon Kuskokwim Health Corp. are also testing wastewater for disease pathogens, he said. Samples are regularly collected from wastewater in Eagle River, Juneau and Bethel. 卫生官员警告俄亥俄州出现 麻疹 暴发 - Spectrum News

Health officials warn of measles outbreak in Ohio - Spectrum News
新闻简报俄亥俄州卫生官员报告了该州 2026 年第三起 麻疹 麻疹,其中阿什塔比拉县和塔斯卡拉瓦斯县有 4 例确诊病例。所有受影响的人均未接种疫苗,年龄从 4 岁到 30 岁不等。这使得俄亥俄州今年的 麻疹 病例总数达到 15 例,而 2025 年为 45 例。麻疹 具有高度传染性,通过空气中的颗粒传播,并且可以在空气中保持传染性长达两个小时。俄亥俄州卫生部正在积极推广 MMR 疫苗接种,其有效率达 97%,并正在调查潜在的暴露情况。卫生当局强调,麻疹 是可以通过 疫苗接种 预防的,敦促居民保护自己和孩子。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-30。据报道,麻疹 是 2026 年的第三次 麻疹 暴发,之前的 暴发 发生在 1 月(凯霍加县)和 2 月(富兰克林县)。
地点与对象
俄亥俄州,特别是阿什塔比拉县和塔斯卡拉瓦斯县。受影响人群包括四名年龄在 4 至 30 岁之间的未接种疫苗的人。
事件
俄亥俄州卫生部 (ODH) 报告了确诊的 麻疹 暴发,定义为三个或更多相关病例。最新的 暴发 中已发现 4 例病例。
规模、影响与风险
本次暴发有四例确诊病例,所有患者均未接种疫苗。2026 年迄今为止,俄亥俄州已发生 15 起 麻疹 病例。麻疹 被描述为极具传染性,通过咳嗽或打喷嚏传播,该病毒能够在空气中存活长达两个小时。
应对、状态与不确定性
ODH 正在推广 疫苗接种 机会,并与卫生部门跟进以调查潜在的暴露情况。暴发得到确认,ODH强调了MMR 疫苗接种的重要性,它对麻疹的有效率为97%。本 暴发 中没有报告死亡或严重并发症。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Health officials warn of measles outbreak in Ohio - Spectrum News

News Brief
Ohio health officials have reported the state's third measles outbreak of 2026, with four confirmed cases in Ashtabula and Tuscarawas Counties. All affected individuals are unvaccinated, ranging from 4 to 30 years old. This brings the total measles cases in Ohio to 15 for the year, compared to 45 in 2025. Measles is highly contagious, spreading through airborne particles, and can remain infectious in the air for up to two hours. The Ohio Department of Health is actively promoting MMR vaccinations, which are 97% effective, and is investigating potential exposures. Health authorities stress that measles is preventable through vaccination, urging residents to protect themselves and their children.
Original source text (English)
OHIO — The Ohio Department of Health (ODH) has reported the state’s third measles outbreak of 2026. An outbreak is defined by three or more related cases. ODH said in this latest outbreak three people in Ashtabula County and one in Tuscarawas County are currently infected. All four people were unvaccinated and ranged from 4- to 30-years-old. ODH is working to promote opportunities to get vaccinated and is following up with health departments to follow up on potential exposures. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” ODH Director Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff said. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Previous outbreaks were reported in January in Cuyahoga County and in February in Franklin County. There have been 15 measles cases so far in 2026. ODH previously reported 45 cases in 2025, seven cases in 2024, and one case in 2023. Measles is extremely contagious and can spread to others through coughing or sneezing. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in the air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If people breathe contaminated air or touch an infected surface, they can become infected. “The measles vaccine (called MMR as it protects against measles, mumps and rubella) is very safe and effective at preventing measles,” ODH said in a news release. “Two doses of MMR is 97% effective against measles. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low.” Symptoms include rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite, and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline before moving to the face and upper neck and then down the body. For more information about measles, visit ODH’s website
View five news elements
Time
Published on 2026-07-30. The outbreak was reported as the third measles outbreak of 2026, with previous outbreaks occurring in January (Cuyahoga County) and February (Franklin County).
Location and population
Ohio, specifically Ashtabula County and Tuscarawas County. The affected population includes four unvaccinated individuals aged 4 to 30 years.
Event
The Ohio Department of Health (ODH) reported a confirmed measles outbreak defined as three or more related cases. Four cases have been identified in this latest outbreak.
Scale, impact and risk
Four confirmed cases in this outbreak, with all patients unvaccinated. So far in 2026, there have been 15 measles cases in Ohio. Measles is described as extremely contagious, spreading through coughing or sneezing, with the virus capable of surviving in the air for up to two hours.
Response, status and uncertainty
ODH is promoting vaccination opportunities and following up with health departments to investigate potential exposures. The outbreak is confirmed, and ODH emphasizes the importance of MMR vaccination, which is 97% effective against measles. No deaths or severe complications are reported in this outbreak.
