安克雷奇废水发出有关 麻疹 的警告

Wastewater in Anchorage signals warning about measles
新闻简报阿拉斯加卫生官员在 2026 年 7 月上旬和中旬的废水样本中检测到 麻疹 RNA 后,向安克雷奇居民发出 麻疹 潜在传播的警告。检测结果表明,该市主要废水 麻疹 工厂服务的区域内至少存在一名感染者。虽然最近没有报告新病例,但官员们强调了由于 疫苗接种 费率较低和旅游旺季旅行增加而带来的风险。卫生当局敦促居民检查他们的 疫苗接种 状态,并在需要时接种疫苗。废水测试提供了一个早期预警系统,但由于测试的复杂性和昂贵性,结果可能会延迟。如果出现疑似病例,建议医疗服务提供者保持警惕并实施适当的隔离方案。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-28。事件发生在2026年7月上旬和中旬,废水样本采集时间为7月7日、8日、9日和14日。
地点与对象
阿拉斯加州安克雷奇,特别是 John M. Asplund 废水 治疗 设施服务的区域。受影响的人口包括安克雷奇居民和其他阿拉斯加人。
事件
确认在废水样本中检测到 麻疹 RNA,表明 2026 年 7 月上旬和中旬,安克雷奇至少有一名 麻疹 患者。州卫生官员对潜在传播发出警告。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年 7 月中旬,安克雷奇至少确诊一例 麻疹 病例。截至周二,没有新报告病例。由于 麻疹 发病率较低,且旅游旺季吸引了来自 麻疹 病例高发地区的旅客,因此传播风险较高。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在监测废水样本并等待最近的检测结果。公共卫生应对措施包括敦促 疫苗接种 和对症状保持警惕。如果发现病例,则需要进行接触者追踪。测试复杂、昂贵且耗时。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wastewater in Anchorage signals warning about measles

News Brief
Alaska health officials are warning Anchorage residents about potential measles spread after detecting measles RNA in wastewater samples from early and mid-July 2026. The detection indicates at least one infected person was present in the area served by the city's main wastewater treatment plant. While no new cases have been reported recently, officials emphasize the risk due to low vaccination rates and increased travel during peak tourist season. Health authorities are urging residents to check their vaccination status and get vaccinated if needed. Wastewater testing provides an early warning system, though results can be delayed due to the complex and expensive nature of the tests. Medical providers are advised to be vigilant and implement proper isolation protocols if cases are suspected.
Original source text (English)
Research professional Nate Beck prepares a sample of wastewater from the Asplund Wastewater Treatment Plant so that it can be tested for SARS-CoV-2 on Feb. 6, 2024 at the University of Alaska Anchorage. Wastewater sampling in Alaska screens for a variety of infectious diseases, including COVID-19, different varieties of influenza and respiratory syncytial virus, or RSV, as well as measles. (Loren Holmes / ADN archive) At least one person in Anchorage had measles in early and mid-July, creating a risk that the highly infectious disease could spread through the state’s largest city, state health officials are warning. The proof is in the sewage . Samples taken from Anchorage’s John M. Asplund Wastewater Treatment Facility revealed genetic material from the strain of measles that has caused outbreaks in the Lower 48. It means someone carrying the virus was in the part of Anchorage served by the state’s biggest wastewater treatment plant, said Dr. Joe McLaughlin, Alaska’s state epidemiologist. “It doesn’t tell us where exactly they were in the sewershed, and it also doesn’t tell us if there was any human-to-human transmission that’s occurring,” he said. But it does provide an early warning to health providers and the general public, McLaughlin said. “It’s important for people to take a look and see if they’ve been vaccinated, or if their children have been vaccinated,” he said. If children have not been vaccinated, “go ahead and get them vaccinated, because we know that this virus has been present in the community. And it may still be present.” The presence of measles RNA, the virus’s single-strand genetic material, was detected in samples taken on July 7, 8, 9 and 14, health officials said. State and municipal health officials are still waiting for results from more recent wastewater testing, McLaughlin said. Samples from the Asplund plant are usually taken every Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday, said Clayton Weingartner, the Division of Public Health’s wastewater surveillance coordinator. One of six clarifiers at the AWWU Asplund Wastewater Treatment Facility at Point Woronzof on Dec. 10, 2024. (Bill Roth / ADN) Performing the scan that detects measles genetic material is a complex process, he said. “It’s an expensive test, and it takes longer to get the results,” he said. That is why state officials are still waiting for results from last week’s sampling, he said. For now, public health officials say, Anchorage residents and other Alaskans should be on the lookout for signs of measles . Symptoms, which usually arrive a week or two after infection, include a high fever, a runny nose, red eyes and a skin rash. The disease can be serious and even fatal, especially for young children. There were three measles deaths in the United States last year, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. As of Tuesday, there were no new reports of any patients with measles in Anchorage. The last Anchorage case was in October, affecting an adult who tested positive after traveling to the city from the Lower 48, according to municipal officials. There was also a child in Anchorage who was diagnosed with measles in May of 2025. Elsewhere in the state, there were four measles cases diagnosed last year, according to health officials. McLaughlin said there are reasons for Alaskans to be vigilant. There are more measles cases in the United States now than at any time in the past 35 years, he said. “And we’re at peak tourist season right now, so there are people coming into Alaska from the Lower 48 and from many different countries, and they’re bringing with them, some of them, different pathogens,” he said. “And one of them can be measles. And we know that measles is the most contagious virus that we’re aware of on the planet.” Additionally, Alaskans are at elevated risk because the rate of measles vaccination is low compared to the rest of the nation. Medical providers should be notified if there are suspected measles infections, McLaughlin said. Patients should not be in clinic waiting rooms because the virus is so contagious, he said. Whenever a case is identified, health officials have to trace the places where that patient may have exposed others to the virus, such as daycare centers, stores and recreational sites. “All that kind of stuff takes a lot of work, and that’s just for one case,” he said. Wastewater sampling in Alaska screens for a variety of infectious diseases, including COVID-19, different varieties of influenza and respiratory syncytial virus, or RSV, as well as measles. Numerous sites participate in the National Wastewater Surveillance System , or NWSS, that was set up by the CDC in 2020 as part of the national response to the COVID-19 pandemic. But the Asplund plant, which serves most of Anchorage’s residents, is the only one in Alaska that participates in the most advanced testing program, a privately operated system called WastewaterSCAN , Weingartner said. “The WastewaterSCAN program has very limited testing slots,” he said. The Alaska Department of Environmental Conservation, the University of Alaska Anchorage and the Yukon Kuskokwim Health Corp. are also testing wastewater for disease pathogens, he said. Samples are regularly collected from wastewater in Eagle River, Juneau and Bethel. Originally published by the Alaska Beacon , an independent, nonpartisan news organization that covers Alaska state government
View five news elements
Time
Published on 2026-07-28. Event occurred in early and mid-July 2026, with wastewater samples taken on July 7, 8, 9, and 14.
Location and population
Anchorage, Alaska, specifically the area served by the John M. Asplund Wastewater Treatment Facility. Affected population includes Anchorage residents and other Alaskans.
Event
Confirmed detection of measles RNA in wastewater samples indicating at least one person with measles was present in Anchorage in early and mid-July 2026. State health officials issued a warning about potential spread.
Scale, impact and risk
At least one confirmed measles case in Anchorage in early-mid July 2026. No new reported cases as of Tuesday. Risk of spread is elevated due to low vaccination rates and peak tourist season bringing travelers from areas with high measles cases.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health officials are monitoring wastewater samples and waiting for recent test results. Public health response includes urging vaccination and vigilance for symptoms. Contact tracing would be required if cases are identified. Testing is complex, expensive, and time-consuming.

锚地废水中检出麻疹病毒;最近没有确诊人类病例 - 《阿拉斯加故事》

Measles virus detected in Anchorage wastewater; no recent human cases confirmed - Alaska Story
新闻简报阿拉斯加州公共卫生官员在 2026 年 7 月 7 日至 14 日收集的安克雷奇废水样本中检测到 麻疹病毒 RNA,但该州最近尚未确认人类病例。7 月 22 日至 24 日,WastewaterSCAN 通知 流行病学 的阿拉斯加部门,称浓度“非常低”,并强调检测结果并不能证实社区是 传播。安克雷奇污水处理厂包括城市和埃尔门多夫-理查森联合基地,但不包括伊格尔河和戈德伍德。已提醒医疗保健提供者注意潜在的 麻疹 病例,特别是旅行者或 麻疹 病史不完整的人,并立即报告疑似病例,无需等待实验室确认。官员强调 麻疹 不会因废水而感染,并鼓励阿拉斯加人验证其 MMR 疫苗接种 状态。截至 7 月 23 日,美国正在经历 35 年来最高的 麻疹 病例数,2026 年确诊病例为 2,318 例。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026 年 7 月 28 日。事件日期:2026 年 7 月 7 日、8 日、9 日和 14 日收集的废水样本中检测到 麻疹病毒 RNA。2026 年 7 月 27 日向医疗保健提供者发出警报。
地点与对象
阿拉斯加州安克雷奇,包括该市和埃尔门多夫-理查森联合基地。下水道棚不包括 Eagle River 或 Girdwood。没有提到具体的人口规模。
事件
在 2026 年 7 月早些时候收集的多个安克雷奇废水样本中检测到 麻疹病毒 RNA。阿拉斯加公共卫生官员于 2026 年 7 月 27 日向医疗保健提供者发出警报。阿拉斯加近期尚未确认 麻疹 人类病例。
规模、影响与风险
病毒浓度被描述为“非常低”。检测结果并未证实 麻疹 正在安克雷奇传播,但表明该病毒在 7 月 7 日至 14 日期间存在于下水道系统中。阿拉斯加尚未报告病例、死亡或暴露情况。截至7月23日,美国2026年已记录2,318例麻疹确诊病例,超过2025年的总数。
应对、状态与不确定性
在宏基因组测序检测到病毒遗传物质后,WastewaterSCAN 于 7 月 22 日至 24 日通知州流行病学家。指示医疗保健提供者对可能的 麻疹 病例保持警惕,特别是旅行者或 麻疹 病史不完整的人。疑似病例应立即进行空气隔离,提供者不应等待实验室确认后再报告。州政府官员强调人们不会从废水中感染 麻疹。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles virus detected in Anchorage wastewater; no recent human cases confirmed - Alaska Story

News Brief
Alaska public health officials detected measles virus RNA in Anchorage wastewater samples collected July 7-14, 2026, though no recent human cases have been confirmed in the state. The Alaska Section of Epidemiology, notified by WastewaterSCAN on July 22-24, described the concentrations as 'very low' and emphasized the detection does not confirm community transmission. The Anchorage sewershed includes the city and Joint Base Elmendorf-Richardson but excludes Eagle River and Girdwood. Healthcare providers have been alerted to watch for potential measles cases, especially among travelers or those with incomplete vaccination histories, and to report suspected cases immediately without waiting for lab confirmation. Officials stress measles cannot be contracted from wastewater and are encouraging Alaskans to verify their MMR vaccination status. The U.S. is experiencing its highest measles case count in 35 years, with 2,318 confirmed cases in 2026 as of July 23.
Original source text (English)
Facebook-f X-twitter Envelope Subscribe Home About 49th State Lower 48 Top News Commentary Northern Ledger Intel 907 Juneau Story Features and events Donate Home About 49th State Lower 48 Top News Commentary Northern Ledger Intel 907 Juneau Story Features and events Donate Home » Measles virus detected in Anchorage wastewater; no recent human cases confirmed 49th State, Top News Measles virus detected in Anchorage wastewater; no recent human cases confirmed Listen By THE ALASKA STORY July 27, 2026 – Measles virus RNA was detected in several Anchorage wastewater samples collected earlier this month, prompting Alaska public health officials to alert healthcare providers Monday. The Alaska Section of Epidemiology said the virus was found in samples collected July 7, 8, 9 and 14. The concentrations were described as “very low,” and no recent human case of measles has been confirmed in Alaska. The finding does not establish that measles is spreading in Anchorage, but it indicates that the virus was present somewhere within the Anchorage sewer system during the July 7–14 period. The Anchorage sewershed includes the city and Joint Base Elmendorf-Richardson but does not include Eagle River or Girdwood. WastewaterSCAN, a national disease-surveillance program, notified state epidemiologists July 22 and 24 after metagenomic sequencing detected the viral genetic material. Wastewater testing can detect viruses, bacteria and other substances shed through toilets, sinks, showers, laundry and drains. It cannot identify the person who carried the virus, establish whether that person was an Anchorage resident or visitor, or determine whether community transmission is occurring. State officials emphasized that people cannot contract measles from wastewater. Measles is a respiratory disease that spreads through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes or breathes. The detection comes as the United States experiences its highest measles case count in 35 years. As of July 23, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention had recorded 2,318 confirmed cases during 2026, already exceeding the 2,289 cases reported during all of 2025. Thirty-five new outbreaks have been reported this year, and 93% of confirmed cases have been linked to outbreaks, according to the CDC. Alaska epidemiologists instructed healthcare providers to remain alert for patients with possible measles, particularly those with recent domestic or international travel, contact with travelers, or incomplete or unknown vaccination histories. Early symptoms can include fever, cough, runny nose and red, watery eyes. Small white spots known as Koplik spots may develop inside the mouth, followed by a red rash that usually begins on the face and spreads downward across the body. Providers who suspect measles are being instructed to place the patient in airborne isolation immediately and notify the Section of Epidemiology at 907-269-8000. The after-hours number is 800-478-0084. Officials said providers should not wait for laboratory confirmation before reporting a suspected case. State health officials are also encouraging Alaskans to check whether they are current on their measles, mumps and rubella vaccinations. Children generally receive two doses of the MMR vaccine, while adults without evidence of immunity should consult a healthcare provider. The CDC says two doses provide the best protection and usually provide lifelong immunity against measles. The wastewater results are not presently displayed on the public WastewaterSCAN dashboard because they were discovered through metagenomic sequencing rather than the standard digital droplet PCR testing used for publicly reportable concentration levels. WastewaterSCAN has indicated that sequencing results may be added to the dashboard in the future. 犹他州-亚利桑那州 麻疹 导致全国 麻疹 病例数量破纪录 - KNAU

Utah-Arizona outbreak contributes to record-breaking number of measles cases nationally - KNAU
新闻简报美国将在 2026 年经历破纪录的 麻疹 暴发,截至 7 月报告病例数为 2,318 例,超过了 1991 年的最高纪录。犹他州和亚利桑那州边境的一次重大 麻疹 对这一激增做出了重大贡献,专家估计,由于计数不足,实际病例数可能比报告的病例数高出三到四倍。南卡罗来纳州和德克萨斯州等其他州也在处理大量 暴发 病例,其中南卡罗来纳州报告了 997 例病例,但可能被低估了。暴发 与 疫苗接种 费率下降有关,该费率已低于防止 传播 所需的 95% 覆盖率。卫生官员正在使用废水测试和数学模型来追踪传播情况,但 监测 和报告中的挑战使得很难确定完整范围。国际卫生官员将于 11 月举行会议,评估美国是否已失去 麻疹 的自由状态。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Utah-Arizona outbreak contributes to record-breaking number of measles cases nationally - KNAU

News Brief
The United States is experiencing a record-breaking measles outbreak in 2026, with 2,318 cases reported as of July, surpassing the previous high in 1991. A major outbreak on the Utah-Arizona border is contributing significantly to this surge, with experts estimating the actual number of cases could be three to four times higher than reported due to undercounting. Other states like South Carolina and Texas are also dealing with substantial outbreaks, with South Carolina reporting 997 cases that are likely an undercount. The outbreak is linked to declining vaccination rates, which have fallen below the 95% coverage needed to prevent transmission. Health officials are using wastewater testing and mathematical modeling to track the spread, but challenges in surveillance and reporting make it difficult to determine the full scope. International health officials will meet in November to assess if the U.S. has lost its measles-free status.

Arizona is part of one of the nation's largest measles outbreaks as the number of cases surpasses the record high set in 2025, with five more months of 2026 still to come. A report from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention cites more than 2,318 cases so far this year — the highest number in more than 30 years. Most of that is attributable to several major outbreaks, including a recent surge on the Arizona-Utah border. Experts have used mathematical modeling and genetic analyses to indicate the outbreak on the Utah-Arizona border could be around three to four times bigger than what’s currently known, Utah epidemiologist Dr. Leisha Nolen said. Health officials say that’s because some infections likely go unreported. The nation had 2,289 cases in 2025, the worst year for measles in more than three decades. Now, 2026 has easily become the U.S.’s worst year for measles since 1991. In addition to the significant outbreak in Utah and Arizona, an outbreak of measles in South Carolina and smaller clusters of cases in other states have sickened hundreds. In November, international health officials will meet to determine if the U.S. and Mexico have lost their measles-free status. The U.S. eliminated local measles spread in 2000 by maintaining high vaccination rates, which have fallen in recent years to below the 95% coverage needed to prevent outbreaks. Measles cases are undercounted While measles cases in the U.S. are climbing, it may be impossible to say exactly how far-reaching the spread is. Public health departments and disease researchers have long acknowledged that official counts are incomplete, because some people get sick and don’t seek out treatment — something not unique to measles. Furthermore, experts say the spread might be greater than what’s known because the virus is one of the most contagious known to medicine and vaccination rates nationwide have declined. National vaccination rates for measles, mumps and rubella — which dropped to 92.5% among kindergartners in the 2024-25 school year, from 95.2% in 2019-20 — obscure even lower rates in certain communities. In Utah, where public health officials have fought measles for more than a year, the virus has sickened more than 700 people during that time, according to the state health department. The picture is muddier in South Carolina, which had the nation’s largest outbreak in 35 years. There, the 997 total is likely an undercount, the state health department has said, but it doesn’t know by how much. KNAU and Arizona News Measles virus detected in Sedona wastewater samples Bree Burkitt , July 14, 2026 Samples from the Sedona Wastewater Treatment Plant tested positive for the measles virus. That indicates that at least one person with a recent infection has been in the area. Listen • 0:43 Dr. Brannon Traxler, acting director of the state department of health, said the undercount is “impossible to know and may vary during the course of an outbreak.” Data gaps can make it hard to reliably estimate an outbreak’s true scope. Experts say hospitalization rates can be a good barometer, but measles hospitalizations are not required to be reported in every state. “If we really had some kind of solid gold standard for the percentage of all cases that have hospitalization or some measure of severity, then that would be another way of estimating the outbreak size that we could see if it’s consistent with these other methods,” said Damon Toth, an applied mathematician at the University of Utah’s medical school who is working on one of the Utah outbreak analyses. Researchers are still analyzing the 2025 Texas outbreak In Texas, researchers are still working to estimate the size of the state’s 2025 outbreak and early findings show it might have been nearly twice the size of the 762 known cases. Rémy Pasco, a mathematical epidemiologist at the University of Colorado Boulder who is working on the analysis, said the undercounts were unsurprising. In the absence of an adequate community vaccination rate — 95% according to health experts — “there’s just not much you can do about measles once you have it,” he said. The CDC defines an outbreak as three or more linked cases. But by the time three such cases are identified in communities with low vaccination rates and less disease surveillance, “either you missed the previous ones or you’re going to have a lot more,” he added. Dr. Varun Shetty, Texas’ chief state epidemiologist, said there were instances where people tested positive for measles but health officials couldn’t confirm who else was in the person’s household or if they were sick. “What we’re able to count and report is almost always an underestimate of what actually occurred, because it relies on people being willing to be able to get tested, to share information with healthcare providers and with public health,” Shetty said. “Sometimes that just doesn’t happen.” The state health department previously reported that 182 probable measles cases went unconfirmed in March 2025 among minors in Gaines County — the epicenter of the outbreak — due to a lack of information. The confirmed case count for the western Texas county for the whole outbreak is 414. Shetty expects to keep seeing measles cases and even outbreaks because so many people are unprotected. “It’s really critical, not only for individual people, but for communities, to have high vaccination coverage,” he said. Vaccination is about protecting others, physician says Dr. Mark Roberts, retired director of the Public Health Dynamics Lab at the University of Pittsburgh School of Public Health, worries most for the people he calls “innocent bystanders.” Research and measles modeling by his team shows those who cannot get vaccinated — young babies, people with weak immune systems and those who rarely don’t get robust immunity from the vaccine — bear the brunt of outbreaks early on. Some people see skipping vaccination as like deciding not to wear a seat belt in a car, Roberts said. “Not vaccinating your kid is like saying, ‘I don’t want my car to have brakes,’” he said. “Because it’s other people that you can hit. It’s not just you in your car. It’s other people.” KNAU and Arizona News Unmapped Route 66: Starlite Lanes thrives as the Mother Road celebrates centennial Adrian Skabelund , July 27, 2026 For seven decades, Flagstaff’s Starlite Lanes has been a bowling beacon in the Flagstaff community along Route 66. Now, its owner looks to pass the torch to the next generation. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

随着全国病例的增加,当地卫生专家警告 麻疹 的危险 - 新闻民主党

Local health experts warn of the dangers of measles as cases rise nationally - The Press Democrat
新闻简报随着 麻疹 病例达到 26 年来的新高,美国各地的卫生官员发出警报,仅 2026 年就报告了 2,318 例病例,比 2000 年宣布消除该疾病以来的任何一年都要多。在加利福尼亚州北湾地区,医生和公共卫生领导人敦促家长遵循科学证据,为孩子接种疫苗,并指出最近 93% 的感染发生在未接种疫苗的个体中。这种具有高度传染性的病毒可以在空气中停留数小时,对婴儿和免疫系统较弱的人构成特别的风险。虽然 2025 年有两名未接种疫苗的儿童死于 麻疹 并发症,但今年尚未报告死亡情况。卫生专家将这种复苏归因于 疫苗接种 率下降,而反 疫苗 运动和政策变化加剧了这种情况,并警告说,免疫率低于 95% 的社区很容易受到快速 暴发 的影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026年7月29日(2026年7月30日更新)。事件时间范围:截至 2026 年 7 月报告的全国 麻疹 病例,历史背景可追溯到 1960 年代。
地点与对象
美国,特别关注北湾地区(加利福尼亚州索诺玛县和纳帕县)。受影响人群:未接种疫苗的个人,特别是12个月以下的儿童和免疫率低于95%的社区。
事件
确认全国 麻疹 卷土重来,2026 年报告了 2,318 例病例(截至上周),超过了前一年的总数 2,289 例,创下了自 2000 年宣布消除 麻疹 以来的最高年度病例数。卫生官员将此归因于 麻疹 发病率下降,并敦促遵守医生建议。
规模、影响与风险
病例:2026 年全国病例数为 2,318 例(超过 2025 年的 2,289 例)。死亡人数:2025年,两名未接种疫苗的儿童死于麻疹相关的急性呼吸衰竭;截至本文发布时,2026 年尚无死亡报告。风险:高,因为 麻疹 具有极高的传染性(可在空气中停留长达两个小时),并且在 麻疹 感染率低于 95% 阈值的社区中快速传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员(索诺玛县卫生官员 Mia Shim 博士、纳帕县公共卫生官员 Christine Wu 博士、加州大学伯克利分校专家 John Swartzberg 博士)正在强调 疫苗接种 的重要性并解决 疫苗 的犹豫不决问题。关于特定 暴发 来源的调查正在进行中。随着学校恢复,未来的 传播 存在不确定性。提供的历史背景:麻疹 于 2000 年在美国被淘汰,但现在又在国内流通。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Local health experts warn of the dangers of measles as cases rise nationally - The Press Democrat

News Brief
Health officials across the United States are sounding alarms as measles cases reach a 26-year high, with 2,318 cases reported in 2026 alone—more than in any year since the disease was declared eliminated in 2000. In California's North Bay region, doctors and public health leaders are urging parents to follow scientific evidence and vaccinate their children, noting that 93% of recent infections occurred in unvaccinated individuals. The highly contagious virus, which can remain airborne for hours, poses particular risks to infants and those with weakened immune systems. While two unvaccinated children died from measles complications in 2025, no fatalities have been reported this year yet. Health experts attribute the resurgence to declining vaccination rates, exacerbated by the anti-vaccine movement and policy changes, warning that communities with immunization rates below 95% are vulnerable to rapid outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
Sarah White comforts her 20 month-old son Landden as he receives the MMR vaccine for measles, mumps and rubella from Maribel Gonzalez, a medical assistant team leader at Santa Rosa Community Health, Tuesday, July 28, 2026, in Santa Rosa. (Kent Porter / The Press Democrat) By Martin Espinoza | martin.espinoza@pressdemocrat.com | The Press Democrat PUBLISHED: July 29, 2026 at 7:04 AM PDT | UPDATED: July 30, 2026 at 2:20 PM PDT Getting your Trinity Audio player ready... Lea el artículo en español aquí. Amid a record rebound of measles cases across the country, North Bay health officials and medical professionals are urging parents to follow the advice of physicians and heed science — not internet-fueled fears — in making important vaccination decisions. While the vast majority of parents in Sonoma and Napa counties have elected to have their children vaccinated against a number of infectious diseases, vaccine hesitancy has left pockets of the nation increasingly vulnerable, according to health experts. The federal Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported last week that U.S. measles cases had hit 2,318 this year so far, more than any recorded year since the disease was declared eliminated in 2000. It’s also more than the 2,289 cases reported in all of 2025. Last year, two unvaccinated children in Texas died of measles-related acute respiratory failure. John Swartzberg, an infectious disease expert at UC Berkeley, said the rise of the anti-vaccine movement, coupled with the Trump administration’s attempts to scale back childhood vaccine recommendations, have resulted in a “marked decline” in the number of people getting vaccinated. “The consequences of that are that that we’ve seen measles re-emerge,” Swartzberg said. “Last year, we had two children die of measles...this year we’re having even more cases. Fortunately we haven’t seen any deaths this year.” Dr. Mia Shim, Sonoma County’s new health officer, called the national trend deeply concerning, given that today’s outbreaks could have been prevented had the country stayed the course it was on a quarter century ago. “The vaccination status in these outbreaks tells an important story,” Shim said. “93% of people who were infected with measles in these outbreaks were unvaccinated.” Shim said the vast majority of today’s outbreaks in the United States have been caused by unvaccinated U.S. residents traveling and then bringing the disease back into local communities where immunization rates have fallen below the 95% threshold that protects the community. When immunization rates fall below that level, the disease can spread very quickly, Shim said. Maribel Gonzalez, a medical assistant team leader at Santa Rosa Community Health, draws up a vaccine, Tuesday, July 28, 2026, in Santa Rosa. (Kent Porter / The Press Democrat) A singles dose vaccine for measles, mumps and rubella at Santa Rosa Community Health, Tuesday, July 28, 2026, in Santa Rosa. (Kent Porter / The Press Democrat) Vaccines ready to be delivered at Santa Rosa Community Health, Tuesday, July 28, 2026, in Santa Rosa. (Kent Porter / The Press Democrat) 1 of 3 Maribel Gonzalez, a medical assistant team leader at Santa Rosa Community Health, draws up a vaccine, Tuesday, July 28, 2026, in Santa Rosa. (Kent Porter / The Press Democrat) Expand Swartzberg said declining vaccination rates have set back the country’s campaign against measles a generation. “We’re back to where we were 30 years ago,” he said. Highly contagious Measles hasn’t been in circulation in Sonoma County since 1993, according to local health officials. The last confirmed case in the county, in 2011, was a traveler from Europe. A case in Napa County, tied to domestic U.S. travel, was confirmed back in January. In the early 1990s, U.S. cases were far fewer than they’ve been in the past two years. By decade, the nation averaged hundreds of thousands of cases in the early to mid-1960s; tens of thousands through the seventies; thousands in the 1980s and hundreds for most the 1990s. Measles was declared eliminated in the U.S. — but not eradicated — in 2000. That meant there was no longer any measles spreading within the country and all new cases were from people contracting the virus abroad. Now, that’s no longer the case, said Dr. Christine Wu, Napa County’s public health officer. “We live in a society where we’re not staying put, we love to travel, especially people in California,” said Wu. “And now it’s not just traveling abroad that can bring measles back into communities but travel within the United States. To me that’s a big concern ahead of school coming back into session.” This table shows the number of measles cases by month that have been detected in California so far in 2026. Source: California Department of Public Health Wu said there are communities in the state as well as Napa County where there’s been an increase in preventable illnesses for which vaccines are available, including whooping cough or pertussis, tetanus, “things you don’t even think about anymore.” For many people today, the seriousness of measles is somewhat of an abstraction. Today’s young parents weren’t alive when U.S. cases numbered in the hundreds of thousands every year. For their parents, however, the disease was well known: In 1962, a year before the measles vaccine was licensed, there were 482,530 cases, according to the CDC. Dr. Irene Baluyut, a pediatrician and chief medical informatics officer at Santa Rosa Community Health, said you don’t have to dig through U.S. medical journals or archived news stories to get some idea of what America’ s pre-vaccination era was like. Baluyut said there are many parts of the world where measles is still a problem. “I grew up in the Philippines and I’ve seen measles,” Baluyut said. “I’ve seen how devastating it is. I’ve seen how fast it travels.” Baluyut said the measles virus is far more contagious than say the influenza or COVID-19 viruses. She said measles can stay airborne and active in a room for up to two hours after an infected person leaves the space. After it has gained hold in an environment shared by unvaccinated people, she said, it spreads very quickly. Children are the most vulnerable to measles-related illnesses, particularly infants under 12 months. Dr. Shim, the Sonoma County health officer stressed that being vaccinated protects infants who may not have received all their shots yet, as well as older people with weakened immune systems who may not be able to receive the vaccine. “Getting vaccinated is one of the important ways we care for one another and prevent measles transmission,” Shim said. Swartzberg, the UC Berkeley infectious disease expert, said measles was historically a serious childhood disease before the vaccine and there was no effective way to treat it. “We just accepted it as something we went through during childhood,” he said, adding that most people survived infection but in some cases there were “tragic consequences.” Measles The measles-mumps-rubella (MMR) vaccine is extremely effective and provides a lifetime of protection. Two doses provide 97% protection against measles. Measles is very contagious. Measles spreads when someone infected speaks, coughs, sneezes or breathes. It can linger in the air up to two hours after the infected person has left. Unvaccinated people are at high risk. Roughly 90% of those unvaccinated and exposed to measles will contract the disease. ​​ Source: California Department of Public Health Getting measles The virus that causes measles enters the “respiratory tree” and mainly causes respiratory symptoms, such as red eyes, runny nose and cough, Swartzberg said. However, the virus also goes throughout the body and can affect people’s immune system, making them susceptible to other infectious diseases for months to even a few years, he said. “But it also affects our central nervous system,” he said. According to the CDC, one of every 1,000 measles infections can lead to brain inflammation or encephalitis, usually within days or weeks of the measles rash appearing. Swartzberg said in such cases measles illness can harm vital parts of the nervous system, including hearing and visual damage. “It can also lead to more serious and more diffuse neurological problems, some of which can be fatal,” he said. “That’s the nervous system component that people often don’t recognize.” Many health health experts trace much of the hesitancy back to a 1998 study by a British physician that claimed a link between the MMR vaccine and autism . That study was entirely debunked and exposed as an elaborate hoax. The Lancet, the medical journal that published the study, fully retracted it after an extensive investigation. Britain’s General Medical Council then stripped the study’s author, Andrew Wakefield, of his medical license. Dr. Adrienne Silver, a pediatrician and director of medical education at Kaiser Permanente Santa Rosa, said she believes parents’ vaccine hesitancy is “fear-based” or “emotion-based.” “If you Google something, you’re going to find something,” she said, referring to the ease with which misinformation can spread. “It’s a decision based on what-if, what if it’s my child,” she said. “Parents want to do the right thing by their children and they worry...it’s really a heartfelt fear that much of the misinformation plays on.” Silver said that no medication is without some level of risk. “If you take Tylenol there’s a risk of an allergic reaction,” she said. “There’s always some degree of risk in almost everything we do, but when you look at the risk benefit, the risks are so much more rare than the benefit of the vaccine.” You can reach Staff Writer Martin Espinoza at 707-521-5213 or martin.espinoza@pressdemocrat.com. Time
Publication date: July 29, 2026 (updated July 30, 2026). Event timeframe: National measles cases reported through July 2026, with historical context dating back to the 1960s.
Location and population
United States, with specific focus on North Bay region (Sonoma and Napa counties, California). Affected population: Unvaccinated individuals, particularly children under 12 months and communities with immunization rates below 95%.
Event
Confirmed national measles resurgence with 2,318 cases reported in 2026 (as of last week), exceeding the previous year's total of 2,289 and marking the highest annual count since measles was declared eliminated in 2000. Health officials attribute this to declining vaccination rates and urge adherence to physician recommendations.
Scale, impact and risk
Cases: 2,318 nationally in 2026 (exceeding 2025's 2,289). Deaths: Two unvaccinated children died of measles-related acute respiratory failure in 2025; no deaths reported in 2026 as of publication. Risk: High due to measles' extreme contagiousness (can remain airborne for up to two hours) and rapid spread in communities with vaccination rates below the 95% threshold.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health officials (Sonoma County Health Officer Dr. Mia Shim, Napa County Public Health Officer Dr. Christine Wu, UC Berkeley expert Dr. John Swartzberg) are emphasizing vaccination importance and addressing vaccine hesitancy. Investigation ongoing regarding specific outbreak sources. Uncertainty exists about future transmission as school resumes. Historical context provided: measles eliminated in U.S. in 2000 but now circulating domestically again.

News Brief
The Pennsylvania Department of Health has confirmed a single measles case in Bedford County, bringing the state's total to 158 cases. Health officials are actively conducting contact tracing for the infected individual. This outbreak has resulted in 28 hospitalizations and 37 new cases reported in the past week. Measles, a highly contagious and vaccine-preventable virus spreads through the air and can lead to severe illness. The case underscores the ongoing public health response to contain the outbreak in Pennsylvania.

健康专家:该州的高 疫苗接种 发病率正在遏制 麻疹 的传播 - 马里兰州事务

Health experts: State’s high vaccination rate is keeping spread of measles in check - Maryland Matters
新闻简报马里兰州卫生官员报告称,尽管 2026 年 麻疹 病例比 2025 年增加了四倍,但该州高达 96% 的 麻疹 发生率阻止了持续的社区 传播。今年迄今为止记录的所有 12 例病例均来自州外或国际旅行,没有本地传播的证据。专家将这一成功归因于强有力的免疫计划,但警告说,幼儿园儿童中越来越多的宗教豁免证明了日益增长的 疫苗 怀疑态度,这构成了未来的风险。由于弗吉尼亚州和宾夕法尼亚州等邻近州经历了严重的 暴发,马里兰州居民被敦促确保接种疫苗,特别是在前往正在发生 传播 的地区时。CDC 报告的 麻疹 病例数量创全国 35 年来新高。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026年7月28日。活动时间范围:2026年1月1日至2026年6月23日(最新案例公布)。
地点与对象
国家:美国。受影响人群:马里兰州居民,特别关注幼儿园儿童(2025-2026 学年入学人数为 65,500 人)。
事件
2026 年前 7 个月,马里兰州确诊的 麻疹 病例增加至 12 例(2025 年全年为 3 例),几乎所有病例都是来自州外或国际旅行。卫生官员报告没有持续的社区 传播,将此归因于马里兰州 96% 的 传播 覆盖率。如果没有高 疫苗接种 率,情况可能会更糟。
规模、影响与风险
病例:2026 年(截至 6 月 23 日)马里兰州确诊 12 例 麻疹 病例,而 2025 年为 3 例。死亡人数:未报告。暴露:未指定。传播:无持续社区传播报道;所有病例均为境外输入。风险:专家警告说,马里兰州也不能幸免于当地的 传播,特别是邻近的州(弗吉尼亚州有 176 例,宾夕法尼亚州有 134 例)也经历了 传播。CDC 报告称,全国 麻疹 病例已达到 35 年来的最高水平(截至 7 月 23 日为 2,318 例)。
应对、状态与不确定性
调查:马里兰州卫生部正在监测病例和 疫苗接种 率。测试:没有明确提及。隔离:未提及。接触者追踪:未提及。预防:卫生官员敦促 疫苗接种,并指出马里兰州的 疫苗接种 覆盖率为 96%(2025-2026 年幼儿园儿童覆盖率为 96.7%)。治疗:没有提到。官方通讯:马里兰州卫生部已发表声明,强调 疫苗接种 的重要性。目前状态:无持续传播;包含输入病例。未解决的信息:最新案例(6 月 23 日公布)的来源未具体说明。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Health experts: State’s high vaccination rate is keeping spread of measles in check - Maryland Matters

News Brief
Maryland health officials report that despite a fourfold increase in measles cases in 2026 compared to 2025, the state's high vaccination rate of 96% has prevented sustained community transmission. All 12 cases recorded so far this year were imported from out-of-state or international travel, with no evidence of local spread. Experts attribute this success to strong immunization programs but warn that growing vaccine skepticism, evidenced by rising religious exemptions among kindergarteners, poses a future risk. As neighboring states like Virginia and Pennsylvania experience significant outbreaks, Maryland residents are urged to ensure they are vaccinated, especially when traveling to areas with ongoing transmission. The CDC has reported measles cases at a 35-year high nationally.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content HOME NEWS Gov & Politics Environment Health Education Justice Transportation Work & the Economy Commentary ABOUT SUBSCRIBE DONATE Part of States Newsroom Search Search Gov & Politics Environment Health Education Justice Transportation Work & the Economy 1:42 News Story Health Health experts: State’s high vaccination rate is keeping spread of measles in check Maryland’s 96% vaccination coverage prevents further spread, as outbreaks in other states put unvaccinated residents at risk By Danielle J. Brown - July 28, 2026 1:42 am Maryland health officials and public health experts urge Marylanders to vaccinate against measles amid nationwide surge in cases. (Photo by Jerry Saslav/U.S. Air Force) Just seven months into the year, confirmed measles cases in Maryland are already four times higher than they were for all of last year, but health officials say the situation could be much worse. Almost all of the 12 cases recorded so far this year — up from three for all of 2025 — were “imported” into Maryland after out-of-state or international travel. But once here, the infections have not spread further, which experts say is a sign that the state’s relatively high vaccine coverage at 96% is protecting residents from the potentially fatal illness. “This is higher than many previous years, but it doesn’t represent sustained transmission within Maryland,” said Dr. William Moss, director of the International Vaccine Access Center at Johns Hopkins University. “We have now had repeated importations into the state without sustained transmission.” It’s a trend that he and public health officials hope to protect amid growing vaccine skepticism as new state data shows more parents opting their children out of required school vaccinations. Approximately 96% of Marylanders have lifelong protection from measles due to being vaccinated, above the national average of 93%, according to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Other Marylanders who have already been sick with measles are also considered immune. The recommend vaccination coverage rate to considered necessary to protect a community from measles is 96%, but as regions across the United States struggle with outbreaks, the dozen cases identified in Maryland reflect those challenges. Of the 12 cases identified by the Maryland Department of Health this year, nine were detected after the resident returned from out-of-state travel, while two cases were detected after international travel. The most recent case, announced June 23 by the health department, did not indicate where the individual may have picked up measles. But Moss says that the lack of sustained community transmission is “a signal of the strength of our immunization program and our really good measles vaccine coverage.” That coverage may be slipping, as more Maryland families cite religious reasons to opt their kids out of vaccinations that are otherwise required to enter kindergarten through religious exemptions. The Maryland Department of Health reports that during the 2025-2026 school year, 2.1% of kindergartners had religious exemptions from the vaccinations, up from 1.7% the year before. That’s more than 1,300 of the 65,500 kindergartners enrolled in the 2025-2026 school year. It’s the highest percentage since 2019-2020, when the religious exemption rate was 2.7% amid disruptions in the school year due to COVID-19. That said, overall vaccine coverage among kindergarteners was still at least 95% in every Maryland county, even in counties where religious exemptions were higher like Allegany and Worcester, according to the health department’s most recent data. “High vaccination rates in schools are crucial for preventing outbreaks and maintaining community immunity,” the Maryland Department of Health said in a written statement Monday. “Maryland maintains a strong safety net with 96.7% of kindergarteners reported as vaccinated in the 2025-2026 school year.” Dr. Andrea Berry is an associate professor of pediatrics at the University of Maryland School of Medicine and a pediatric infectious disease specialist at University of Maryland Golisano Children’s Hospital. She says that over the last few years, there’s been growing confusion and vaccine skepticism among parents, which leads them to opt their kids out of vaccines, including the measles vaccine. “There is a very close relationship between vaccination rates and the likelihood of an outbreak,” she said. “Overall, Maryland does a really great job getting children vaccinated, but the increase in these exemptions stems from vaccine hesitancy.” Meanwhile, the number of measles cases climbs across the United States with 2,318 cases confirmed as of July 23 this year. Those cases have reached 44 states and Washington, D.C., with Maryland’s neighbors among the states with the largest measles outbreaks currently, with Virginia facing 176 measles cases and Pennsylvania facing 134 cases. Measles cases have reached a 35-year high, CDC reports Despite Virginia’s high vaccination rate at 96%, the measles outbreak it’s facing occurred in two rural counties where vaccination rates are less than 75%. Meanwhile, Pennsylvania’s statewide vaccination rate is 92%. Moss warns that the growing measles cases in neighboring states create a greater chance of exposure when Marylanders are visiting those regions, especially in communities with lower vaccination coverage. He says Maryland is “not immune to having local transmission.” “I suspect that we have communities here in Maryland that have lower vaccine coverage ... but fortunately, these imported cases have not made it into those communities yet,” he said. “Traveling to a place where there’s ongoing measles virus transmission ... the most important thing is to make sure you’re protected against measles.” Berry noted that anyone past the age of 6 months can get a measles vaccination. She urges Marylanders to get vaccinated if they are not already protected through prior illness from measles, especially as cases rise in neighboring states. “Now that there are so many outbreaks all throughout the United States, it’s more likely that somebody is going to travel to those places or speak to somebody who did travel those places,” she said. “With the vaccine, you’re much less likely to get measles, and when people do get measles, it is not as severe.” 1 X Republish Post Health experts: State’s high vaccination rate is keeping spread of measles in check by Danielle J. Brown, Maryland Matters July 28, 2026 <h2>Health experts: State’s high vaccination rate is keeping spread of measles in check</h2> <p>by Danielle J. Brown, <a href="https://marylandmatters.org">Maryland Matters</a> <br />July 28, 2026</p> <p>Just seven months into the year, confirmed measles cases in Maryland are already four times higher than they were for all of last year, but health officials say the situation could be much worse.</p> <p>Almost all of the 12 cases recorded so far this year — up from three for all of 2025 — were “imported” into Maryland after out-of-state or international travel. But once here, the infections have not spread further, which experts say is a sign that the state’s relatively high vaccine coverage at 96% is protecting residents from the potentially fatal illness.</p> <p>“This is higher than many previous years, but it doesn’t represent sustained transmission within Maryland,” said Dr. William Moss, director of the International Vaccine Access Center at Johns Hopkins University. “We have now had repeated importations into the state without sustained transmission.”</p> <p>It’s a trend that he and public health officials hope to protect amid growing vaccine skepticism as new state data shows more parents opting their children out of required school vaccinations.</p> <p>Approximately 96% of Marylanders have lifelong protection from measles due to being vaccinated, above the national average of 93%, according to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Other Marylanders who have already been sick with measles are also considered immune.</p> <p>The recommend vaccination coverage rate to considered necessary to protect a community from measles is 96%, but as regions across the United States struggle with outbreaks, the dozen cases identified in Maryland reflect those challenges.</p> <p>Of the 12 cases identified by the Maryland Department of Health this year, nine were detected after the resident returned from out-of-state travel, while two cases were detected after international travel. The most recent case, announced June 23 by the health department, did not indicate where the individual may have picked up measles.</p> <div class="halfwidth "> <iframe height="373" title="Percent of kindergarteners with religious exemptions from required vaccines by academic year" src="https://datawrapper.dwcdn.net/SpzYz/3/" width="600" frameborder="0"></iframe> </div> <p>But Moss says that the lack of sustained community transmission is “a signal of the strength of our immunization program and our really good measles vaccine coverage.”</p> <p>That coverage may be slipping, as more Maryland families cite religious reasons to opt their kids out of vaccinations that are otherwise required to enter kindergarten through religious exemptions. The Maryland Department of Health reports that during the 2025-2026 school year, 2.1% of kindergartners had religious exemptions from the vaccinations, up from 1.7% the year before.</p> <p>That’s more than 1,300 of the 65,500 kindergartners enrolled in the 2025-2026 school year. It’s the highest percentage since 2019-2020, when the religious exemption rate was 2.7% amid disruptions in the school year due to COVID-19.</p> <p>That said, overall vaccine coverage among kindergarteners was still at least 95% in every Maryland county, even in counties where religious exemptions were higher like Allegany and Worcester, according to the health department’s most recent data.</p> <p>“High vaccination rates in schools are crucial for preventing outbreaks and maintaining community immunity,” the Maryland Department of Health said in a written statement Monday. “Maryland maintains a strong safety net with <a href="https://health.maryland.gov/phpa/OIDEOR/IMMUN/Shared%20Documents/rate_tables/2025-26%20Kindergarten%20Vaccine%20Coverage%20Rates_Final.pdf">96.7% of kindergarteners</a> reported as vaccinated in the 2025-2026 school year.”</p> <p>Dr. Andrea Berry is an associate professor of pediatrics at the University of Maryland School of Medicine and a pediatric infectious disease specialist at University of Maryland Golisano Children’s Hospital. She says that over the last few years, there’s been growing confusion and vaccine skepticism among parents, which leads them to opt their kids out of vaccines, including the measles vaccine.</p> <p>“There is a very close relationship between vaccination rates and the likelihood of an outbreak,” she said. “Overall, Maryland does a really great job getting children vaccinated, but the increase in these exemptions stems from vaccine hesitancy.”</p> <p>Meanwhile, the number of measles cases climbs across the United States with 2,318 cases confirmed as of July 23 this year. Those cases have reached 44 states and Washington, D.C., with Maryland’s neighbors among the states with the largest measles outbreaks currently, with Virginia facing 176 measles cases and Pennsylvania facing 134 cases.</p> <blockquote><p><a href="https://marylandmatters.org/2026/07/26/repub/measles-cases-have-reached-a-35-year-high-cdc-reports/">Measles cases have reached a 35-year high, CDC reports</a></p></blockquote> <p><iframe title="“Measles cases have reached a 35-year high, CDC reports” — Maryland Matters" src="https://marylandmatters.org/2026/07/26/repub/measles-cases-have-reached-a-35-year-high-cdc-reports/embed/#?secret=9JUiOCiLVl#?secret=87NYKehWHE" width="500" height="282" frameborder="0"></iframe></p> <p>Despite Virginia’s high vaccination rate at 96%, the measles outbreak it’s facing occurred in two rural counties where vaccination rates are less than 75%. Meanwhile, Pennsylvania’s statewide vaccination rate is 92%.</p> <p>Moss warns that the growing measles cases in neighboring states create a greater chance of exposure when Marylanders are visiting those regions, especially in communities with lower vaccination coverage. He says Maryland is “not immune to having local transmission.”</p> <p>“I suspect that we have communities here in Maryland that have lower vaccine coverage ... but fortunately, these imported cases have not made it into those communities yet,” he said. “Traveling to a place where there’s ongoing measles virus transmission ... the most important thing is to make sure you’re protected against measles.”</p> <p>Berry noted that anyone past the age of 6 months can get a measles vaccination. She urges Marylanders to get vaccinated if they are not already protected through prior illness from measles, especially as cases rise in neighboring states.</p> <p>“Now that there are so many outbreaks all throughout the United States, it’s more likely that somebody is going to travel to those places or speak to somebody who did travel those places,” she said. “With the vaccine, you’re much less likely to get measles, and when people do get measles, it is not as severe.”</p> <style> figure, .tipContainer, .socContainer, .subscribeShortcodeContainer, .donateContainer {display:none !important;} .youtubeContainer { position: relative; padding-bottom: 56.25%; padding-top: 30px; height: 0; overflow: hidden; margin-bottom:12px; } .youtubeContainer iframe, .video-container object, .video-container embed { position: absolute; top: 0; left: 0; width: 100% !important; height: 100%; margin: 12px 0px !important; } .newsroomSidebar {width:35%;max-width:35%;padding:10px;border-top:solid 2px black;background-color:#d3d3d3;float:right;margin-left:50px;} .snrsInfoboxSubContainer {padding:10px;border-top:solid 2px black;background-color:#d3d3d3;} .halfwidth {float:right;width:50%;max-width:50%;} .indent2Container {margin-left: 1em;margin-bottom:1em; border-left: solid 1px black;padding-left: 2em;} @media only screen and (max-width: 600px) {.newsroomSidebar {max-width:95%;width:95%;margin-left:4%} .halfwidth {float:none;width:100%;max-width:100%;} }</style> <p><a href="https://marylandmatters.org">Maryland Matters</a> is part of States Newsroom, a nonprofit news network supported by grants and a coalition of donors as a 501c(3) public charity. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ohio reports 3rd measles outbreak of 2026; US cases hit 35-year high - beaconjournal.com

News Brief
Ohio is reporting its third measles outbreak of 2026, with four unvaccinated individuals infected in Ashtabula and Tuscarawas Counties. This adds to 15 total cases in the state so far, following outbreaks in Cuyahoga and Franklin Counties. Nationally, measles cases have surged to a 35-year high of 2,295 in 2026, exceeding the previous year's total. Health officials link the rise to declining vaccination rates, with US kindergartener coverage falling below the 95% herd immunity threshold to 92.5%. Ohio's MMR coverage was 91.5% in 2023. Officials stress vaccination as the primary prevention measure, as measles remains highly contagious with potentially severe complications.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Entertainment Lifestyle Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals NEWS Ohio reports 3rd measles outbreak of 2026; US cases hit 35-year high Chad Murphy USA TODAY NETWORK July 31, 2026, 2:23 p.m. ET Ohio is currently experiencing its third measles outbreak of 2026, with all infected individuals being unvaccinated. The latest Ohio outbreak includes four infected individuals in Ashtabula and Tuscarawas Counties. Nationwide, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, surpassing totals from the previous year. Health officials attribute the rise in cases to declining vaccination rates, which have fallen below the 95% threshold for herd immunity. Ohio is reporting its third measles outbreak of 2026 as cases nationwide hit a 35-year high for a disease once declared eradicated in the United States. The latest outbreak, defined as three or more related cases, includes four infected individuals — three in Ashtabula County and one in Tuscarawas County — ranging in age from 4 to 30, the Ohio Department of Health announced July 30. All four were unvaccinated. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Nationwide, data released July 22 by the Johns Hopkins University shows 2,295 measles cases have been reported nationwide so far this year, surpassing the 2,286 infections recorded during all of 2025 and marking the highest annual total since 1991, USA TODAY reports. Measles cases in Ohio growing So far in 2026, ODH is reporting 15 measles cases and two previous outbreaks – three cases in Cuyahoga County in January and seven cases in Franklin County in February, according to the state's Summary of Infectious Diseases dashboard. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with 45 in 2025, seven cases in 2024 and one case in 2023. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, USA TODAY reports. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Health experts say the recent surge has been fueled by declining vaccination rates that have left more communities vulnerable to outbreaks. Why are measles cases on the rise? Ohio vaccination rates Vaccination for measles has declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated and most people are protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–2020 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–2025 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventives, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023, the most recent year available from ODH. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Symptoms Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before, through four days after, a rash appears. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. 迄今为止,美国报告的 麻疹 病例已超过 2,000 例,超过 2025 年的总数 - NPR

More than 2,000 measles cases reported in the U.S. so far, outpacing 2025's total - NPR
新闻简报美国正在经历 麻疹 的显着复苏,2026 年迄今已报告确诊病例超过 2,300 例,超过上一年的总数。根据 NPR 引用的 CDC 数据,麻疹 代表美国可能失去 2000 年实现的 麻疹 消除状态。流行病学家 Jennifer Nuzzo 博士将这一激增归因于 疫苗接种 发病率下降,而这种下降是由有关 疫苗 安全性和有效性的政治错误信息推动的。麻疹 是一种高度传染性且可能致命的疾病,正在导致住院治疗并使医疗保健系统紧张。暴发 的特点是在多个状态中重复出现 暴发,并且没有停止的迹象。公共卫生专家强调,疫苗接种 仍然是最有效的预防方法,尽管卫生官员发出的令人困惑的信息使应对工作变得复杂。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

More than 2,000 measles cases reported in the U.S. so far, outpacing 2025's total - NPR

News Brief
The United States is experiencing a significant measles resurgence with over 2,300 confirmed cases reported so far in 2026, exceeding the total for the previous year. According to CDC data cited by NPR, this outbreak represents a potential loss of the U.S. measles elimination status achieved in 2000. Epidemiologist Dr. Jennifer Nuzzo attributes the surge to declining vaccination rates fueled by political misinformation about vaccine safety and efficacy. Measles, a highly contagious and potentially fatal disease, is causing hospitalizations and straining healthcare systems. The outbreak is characterized by recurring outbreaks across multiple states with no signs of stopping. Public health experts emphasize that vaccination remains the most effective prevention method, though confusing messaging from health officials has complicated response efforts.
Original source text (English)

Health More than 2,000 measles cases reported in the U.S. so far, outpacing 2025's total July 27, 20264:40 AM ET Heard on Morning Edition A Martínez More than 2,000 measles cases reported in the U.S. so far, outpacing 2025's total Listen· 3:473-Minute ListenPlaylist Transcript Toggle more options Download Embed Embed <iframe src="https://www.npr.org/player/embed/nx-s1-5906455/nx-s1-9864190" width="100%" height="290" frameborder="0" scrolling="no" title="NPR embedded audio player"> Transcript NPR's A Martinez asks Dr. Jennifer Nuzzo of Brown University why there have been more measles cases this year so far than in all of last year, and what can be done to stop the resurgence. Transcript A MARTÍNEZ, HOST: There are more than 2,300 confirmed cases of measles in the U.S. so far this year. That is slightly more than the total number of cases for all of last year, according to the CDC. Dr. Jennifer Nuzzo has been tracking this year's spike in measles. She's an epidemiologist who heads the Pandemic Center at the Brown University School of Public Health. Dr., measles was officially eliminated from the U.S. in 2000. How did this resurgence happen? JENNIFER NUZZO: Well, we've been seeing a slow progress towards measles circulating again because people are, you know, not vaccinating their kids as much as they used to be. But really, it's taken off quite extraordinarily since the beginning of 2025. And some of that is because of a lot of rhetoric right now that's been happening politically about vaccines and suggesting that they may not be safe, which is not true, suggesting that they may not work, which is definitely not true. And that has led to explosive outbreaks throughout the country - recurring outbreaks - to the point where we are now looking at measles being reestablished as a disease that kids could get if they're not vaccinated. MARTÍNEZ: So you expect the U.S. will lose that eliminated status? NUZZO: By all measure, we should lose that status. Measles has continued to be transmitted since the beginning of 2025. It shows no signs of stopping. States that had an outbreak that burnt itself out have seen outbreaks again. It's basically like Whac-A-Mole at this point. MARTÍNEZ: All right. And just to be clear, eliminated means a disease has gone from a region, eradicated it means it's a global - it's gone completely, right? NUZZO: Yeah, it basically means you drive your domestic transmission down to zero. We still had measles when we - since we eliminated it in the year 2000, but they were sporadic cases imported when people traveled to other places. MARTÍNEZ: Why is this resurgence in measles cases so serious? NUZZO: Well, measles is a really terrible disease. You don't want to get measles, you don't want your children to get measles. First of all, it's a deadly disease. It kills, you know, a decent number of people. It puts a lot of people in the hospital, and that's bad for everybody. It's bad for the people who are there with measles. It's also bad for patients who now have a hospital that has to deal with the stress of measles. It's bad for societies because it costs tens of thousands of dollars per measles case. And if your child has measles as a toddler and recovers, you still have to worry about a very rare but fatal complication that can occur as much as a decade later. And that's a really terrible state to be in. MARTÍNEZ: If someone is vaccinated from the measles or their whole families are vaccinated, are they safe? Are they OK? NUZZO: Yeah, so the great thing about the measles vaccine is it's one of the best vaccines we have. It's one of the safest vaccines we have. It's also one of the most effective. And it really does a great job of keeping people from getting sick with measles, certainly keeping people out of the hospital, keeping them from dying, which is the most important thing, but it does a pretty great job of reducing transmission, and that's how we have been able to eliminate it more than 25 years ago. MARTÍNEZ: The Senate is considering the nomination of Dr. Erika Schwartz to lead the CDC. Her boss would be Health Secretary RFK Jr. He's pushed some conspiracy theories about vaccines. Quickly, Dr., if you can, if she is confirmed - if Dr. Schwartz is confirmed - what does she have to do to reverse the rise of measles? NUZZO: She has to issue an unequivocable statement that supports measles vaccination as the only way to protect people from measles - that it's the only proven way to protect yourself from measles. We can't mince words here. We can't suggest that there are, you know, more cons than there are pros. None of that is true. And unfortunately, you know, confusing rhetoric coming out of the Department of Health and Human Services has complicated the issue in the midst of a deadly outbreak. MARTÍNEZ: Dr. Jennifer Nuzzo directs the Pandemic Center at the Brown University School of Public Health. Dr., thanks. NUZZO: Thanks so much. (SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC) Copyright © 2026 NPR. B routine priorityPublic-health news

North Texas doctors warn parents amid rising measles cases - CBS News

News Brief
North Texas doctors are urging parents to ensure their children are vaccinated against measles before the school year begins, as the U.S. faces its worst measles outbreak in over 30 years. With over 2,300 cases reported nationwide this year—the highest since 1991—and four confirmed in Texas this week, health officials emphasize the virus's extreme contagiousness and severity. Measles, once eliminated in the U.S., is making a comeback, with declining vaccination rates cited as a key factor. In North Texas, some school districts fall below the recommended 95% immunity threshold, raising concerns about potential outbreaks. Doctors warn that one-third of infected children may become severely ill, with a death rate of 5-10%. Health authorities stress the importance of timely vaccination, as it takes about two weeks for protection to take effect.

Read Full Bio Amelia Mugavero July 29, 2026 / 10:34 PM CDT / CBS Texas Add CBS News on Google North Texas doctors are urging parents to check their children's measles vaccination records before school starts, as the U.S. faces its worst measles outbreak in more than 30 years. Just weeks before students return to classrooms, measles – a virus once considered eliminated in the United States – is making a comeback. More than 2,300 cases have been reported nationwide this year, the highest count since 1991, and four cases were confirmed in Texas this week. Internal medicine physician Dr. David Winter with Baylor Scott & White says measles spreads with extraordinary ease. "It's said to be the most contagious virus on the planet," Winter said. CBS News Texas He warns that the illness can be severe for children. "If a child gets the measles, about a third of them will get very, very sick. Very sick," Winter said. "The death rate can be up to 5-10% in kids." Winter says declining vaccination rates are a major factor behind the rise in cases. "The vaccine is safe for children," Winter said. "So, in my view, all children should be vaccinated. It's a preventable disease." Doctors say the biggest concern is when measles reaches communities with lower vaccination rates – and new state data shows some North Texas districts are falling below recommended levels. About 93 percent of Texas kindergarteners were fully vaccinated against measles last school year, but several districts fell short. Dallas ISD's MMR vaccination rate for kindergarteners was about 82 percent, while Denton, Celina, and LoveJoy ISDs were all below 90 percent. Health experts generally recommend about 95 percent immunity in a community to help prevent outbreaks. Dallas County Health Director Dr. Philip Huang points to a recent outbreak as a reminder of the stakes. "Last year, in West Texas, there was a record measles outbreak just in Texas, and, you know, and there were two child deaths," Huang said. "And, you know, that should wake people up on how this is really serious. And, so, you know, getting these messages out." Huang says the MMR vaccine takes about two weeks to provide protection, meaning parents shouldn't wait until the first day of school to make sure their children are up to date. Symptoms can appear within days after exposure and may include moderate fever, cough, runny nose, red eyes, and sore throat. "It's a safe and effective vaccine that's worked for decades, and it's a reason why the United States declared measles eliminated in 2000, and now we're really backtracking on it," Huang said. Event
Confirmed measles outbreak in the U.S., described as the worst in over 30 years. Four cases confirmed in Texas this week. Doctors are urging parents to check vaccination records before school starts. The outbreak is linked to declining vaccination rates.

Two child deaths were reported in a previous Texas outbreak. Vaccination rates in some North Texas districts are below recommended levels (Dallas ISD at 82%, others below 90%).
Response, status and uncertainty

Michigan health officials urge vaccinations as measles threat looms - The Detroit News
新闻简报密歇根州卫生官员敦促家庭确保 麻疹 是最新的,因为该州将在 2026 年面临 麻疹 麻疹 确诊病例,其中有 14 例确诊病例。与此同时,美国报告了 35 年来最高的 麻疹 病例,截至 7 月 23 日已确诊 2,318 例。密歇根州在沃什特诺出现了 麻疹 病例。和渥太华县,马科姆县和门罗县有更多病例。卫生官员强调了 疫苗接种 的重要性,因为接种率已经下降,只有 76% 的学龄前儿童和 70% 的幼儿园儿童完全接种了 MMR 疫苗。该州成功控制了最近的 暴发,但警告称仍有可能出现更多病例。随着学年临近,官员们正在通过“我疫苗接种”活动推广 疫苗接种。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 30 日。事件涉及 2026 个 麻疹 案例以及截至 2026 年 7 月持续存在的问题。
地点与对象
美国密歇根州,特别是出现 暴发 的沃什特诺县和渥太华县,以及出现孤立病例的马科姆县和门罗县。受影响人群包括家庭、学生、学龄前儿童(19-35个月)和幼儿园适龄儿童(4-5岁)。
事件
密歇根州2026年确诊14例麻疹 麻疹(沃什特诺县7例,渥太华县4例,马科姆县2例,门罗县1例)。密歇根州卫生官员在美国 麻疹 病例不断增加(截至 2026 年 7 月 23 日为 2,318 例)和 疫苗接种 发病率下降的情况下敦促使用 疫苗接种。
规模、影响与风险
密歇根州2026年有14例麻疹确诊病例,2025年有30例。截至2026年7月23日,已有2,318例麻疹确诊病例,为35年来最高。麻疹 被描述为具有很强的传染性,有可能导致严重的健康并发症。由于 疫苗接种 率下降(76% 的学龄前儿童和 70% 的幼儿园儿童完全接种了 MMR 疫苗,近年来分别下降了 10% 和 6%),因此风险加大。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在敦促 疫苗接种 并开展 暴发 控制工作。当地卫生部门已努力尽量减少影响,自春季以来没有报告重大活动。官员们指出,仍有机会出现更多案件。I Vaccinate 活动正在提高 疫苗接种 意识。未来的病例数和 疫苗接种 的吸收率仍存在不确定性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Michigan health officials urge vaccinations as measles threat looms - The Detroit News

News Brief
Michigan health officials are urging families to ensure vaccinations are up-to-date as the state faces a measles outbreak with 14 confirmed cases in 2026. This comes as the U.S. reports its highest measles cases in 35 years, with 2,318 cases confirmed as of July 23. Michigan has seen outbreaks in Washtenaw and Ottawa counties, with additional cases in Macomb and Monroe counties. Health officials emphasize the importance of vaccination as rates have declined, with only 76% of preschoolers and 70% of kindergarteners fully vaccinated for MMR. The state successfully controlled recent outbreaks but warns that additional cases are still possible. Officials are promoting vaccination through the I Vaccinate campaign as the school year approaches.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Autos Business Michigan Life + Home Entertainment Opinion Obituaries MICHIGAN Michigan health officials urge vaccinations as measles threat looms Anne Snabes The Detroit News July 30, 2026, 12:57 p.m. ET Hear this story View Comments Michigan health officials on Thursday urged families to check their vaccination records and catch up on missed vaccines ahead of the school year as the United States logs the most measles cases in 35 years and a comeback of whooping cough is happening. Michigan has seen 14 cases of measles this year, most of which were associated with outbreaks in Washtenaw and Ottawa counties, and the state confirmed 30 cases in 2025. But the state isn't fully through 2026 yet, said Ryan Malosh, director of the Division of Immunization at the Michigan Department of Health and Human Services. "There's still opportunities for there to be additional cases," Malosh said. At a virtual roundtable on Thursday, Dr. Natasha Bagdasarian, the state's chief medical executive, said the U.S. is experiencing the worst year for measles since 1991. She said the country's number of measles cases this year has already surpassed the total for 2025. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said 2,318 confirmed measles cases had been reported in the U.S. this year as of July 23. Measles is a very contagious virus and can cause serious health complications, the CDC said. The I Vaccinate campaign, a public-private partnership that provides parents with information about vaccinations, held the roundtable on Thursday with health officials and advocates for vaccination. As the start of the school year approaches, Bagdasarian urged parents to check their children's vaccination records to ensure they don't have any outstanding immunizations and asked them to follow the American Academy of Pediatrics' immunization schedule. Rachel VanDenBrink, a Kent Intermediate School District nurse consultant and Michigan Director of the National Association of School Nurses, said that when families choose to vaccinate, the impact "reaches far beyond a single shot." "It protects ... your student from severe illness and lost learning time," VanDenBrink said. "It creates a safe environment in the school for vulnerable classmates who have chronic health conditions and rely on that community immunity to attend school safely. It protects the younger siblings at home who are still too young to be fully vaccinated." Measles in Michigan this year Michigan saw outbreaks of measles earlier this year, including an outbreak with seven cases in Washtenaw County and one with four cases in Ottawa County, according to the state health department. The state has also reported three cases this year that weren't associated with outbreaks: two in Macomb County and one in Monroe County. Malosh said Michigan has not seen "a lot of activity" since the spring. "Through the dedicated efforts of our local health department staff ― really, I mean, hours and hours and hours of staff time dedicated to controlling these outbreaks ― we were able to minimize the impact, and we haven't seen many additional cases this year," he said. Malosh said the state confirmed 30 cases of measles in 2025, but it didn't report any in each year from 2019 through 2024. But in 2018, Michigan reported around 38 or 39 cases, he said. Vaccination rates have declined About 76% of kids in Michigan in the preschool age group ― 19- to 35-month-olds ― have received the recommended doses of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine, Malosh said. This represents an about 10% drop over the last five years, he said About 70% of kindergarten-aged kids in Michigan have had the two recommended doses of MMR vaccine, Malosh said. This tends to mean children ages 4 and 5 years old. Bagdasarian also noted that pertussis, or whooping cough, is "making a comeback." B routine priorityPublic-health news

Florida ranks 5th in the country for the most measles cases - Spectrum News 13

News Brief
Florida ranks fifth nationally for measles cases, with 141 recorded by the CDC near the end of July 2026, marking the highest number since 1992. The outbreak includes confirmed cases in Collier County, with Orange County reporting three cases in the past 12 years. Florida's vaccination rate of 88.8% for kindergarteners is below the national average. Pediatrician Hernando Cardona, who has witnessed measles in Colombia, described its severity, noting rare but serious complications like encephalitis. Surgeon General Joseph Ladapo emphasized public rights to exemptions while supporting vaccine efficacy. Health officials continue monitoring the situation, with vaccines showing 93-99% effectiveness.
Original source text (English)
ORLANDO, Fla. — Florida ranks fifth in the nation for the most measles cases, according to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. On July 24, the CDC announced the highest number of cases the country has seen since 1992. Dr. Hernando Cardona, who runs Pediatrics of Greater Orlando, said he hasn’t seen a case of measles at his clinic, but he witnessed the disease while working in Colombia. “It was pretty bad,” he said. “Most cases don’t go into encephalitis or anything like this, but when it does, it’s bad.” Florida has a lower rate of vaccination than most states, with 88.8% of kindergarteners inoculated against measles in 2025, according to the CDC’s most recent data. The CDC recorded 141 cases in Florida near the end of July. “I am an advocate for the vaccine, but if some families don’t want to do the vaccine, it’s well within their rights,” Cardona said. Florida Surgeon General Joseph Ladapo was in Orlando on July 24 and spoke about the outbreak in Collier County. He emphasized people’s right to exemptions but backed the vaccine. “This one happens to be effective against measles, and people should be able to have the information about the vaccine and about the disease and be able to come to a decision without any coercion,” he said. Orange County has only seen four cases in the past 10 years, according to Florida Department of Health data , but three were confirmed in the past 12 months. Cardona said the first dose children get when they’re around 12 months old is 93% effective, and the second dose they get going into kindergarten is 99% effective
View five news elements
Time
Published on 2026-07-30. Event date: July 24, 2026, when CDC announced the highest number of cases since 1992.
Location and population
United States, with Florida ranking 5th. Specific locations include Orlando (Hernando Cardona's clinic), Collier County (outbreak), and Orange County (4 cases in past 10 years, 3 in past 12 months). Affected population: kindergarteners (88.8% vaccination rate in 2025).
Event
Confirmed measles outbreak. CDC reported 141 cases in Florida near the end of July 2026. Florida ranks 5th nationally for measles cases. Surgeon General Joseph Ladapo spoke in Orlando on July 24 about the Collier County outbreak.
Scale, impact and risk
141 cases recorded in Florida by CDC. Most cases do not progress to encephalitis, but when they do, outcomes are severe. Florida has a lower vaccination rate (88.8% for kindergarteners) than most states. Risk level not explicitly stated.
Response, status and uncertainty
Investigation and response ongoing. Cardona advocates for vaccines but acknowledges families' rights to exemptions. Ladapo emphasizes informed decision-making without coercion. Vaccine effectiveness: 93% after first dose (12 months), 99% after second dose (kindergarten). Orange County data shows localized impact.
