Add Axios on Google Data: CDC; Map: Sara Wise / Axios Virginia is one of the seven states accounting for the majority of U.S. measles cases as kids prepare to head back to school. Why it matters: The nationwide measles case count has officially shattered last year's record, hitting levels unseen in more than three decades, according to CDC data released Friday. The big picture: Virginia has reported at least 177 measles cases, per the latest state Department of Health update Tuesday. That is 35 times higher than the total recorded for 2025: five cases. It's also the fourth-highest case count recorded among states, trailing only Texas (803), South Carolina (670) and Utah (197). Zoom in: Nearly every case in Virginia is among unvaccinated people, and most (66%) are minors. And although Central Virginia is a hotspot, there haven't been any measles cases reported in the Richmond area. The outbreaks, so far, are largely in Buckingham and Cumberland. By the numbers: The CDC recommends two doses of the measles vaccine for children by age 6 and a 95% vaccination coverage rate for the highest level of protection against spread. But recent VDH data updated last week shows the two-dose rate for 5-year-olds statewide is only 72% — though the rate rises to 87% by age 7. It's far lower in Buckingham and Cumberland counties for both age groups. Among rates for 5-year-olds in the region, Richmond (85%), Goochland (78%), Henrico (73%) and Chesterfield (73%) are above 70% — a change from earlier this year when only the city and Petersburg were above that threshold. Some of the lowest rates: Hanover (65%) and Petersburg (69%). Both are lower than the rates reported in February. The intrigue: First-dose coverage in Virginia remains much higher, a pattern that's continued with about 95% of 5-year-olds meeting that mark statewide, per VDH. Locally, Richmond, Henrico, Petersburg and Goochland are the only ones to hit that same 95% coverage. Reality check: The biggest risk remains for unvaccinated people and communities with lower vaccination rates, but health officials have advised residents to skip large, crowded events in areas having outbreaks. Measles cases are surging in Pennsylvania - Axios

News Brief
Pennsylvania is experiencing a significant measles surge, with 142 cases reported through July 27, 2026—marking the state's highest annual total in at least 25 years. Lancaster County is the epicenter, accounting for roughly 60% of cases, followed by Lebanon and Chester counties. All cases involve unvaccinated individuals, and 26 hospitalizations have occurred, though no deaths have been reported. The outbreak aligns with a national trend, as the U.S. has recorded 2,318 measles cases—a 35-year high—with most cases among unvaccinated people. Pennsylvania's 92.4% student vaccination rate in 2025 falls short of the 95% herd immunity threshold, fueling spread. The state health department has responded by launching an online dashboard to monitor cases.

Add Axios on Google Illustration: Lindsey Bailey/Axios Measles cases are surging in Pennsylvania and across the U.S. to levels not seen in years. Why it matters: The virus is rebounding in communities with lower vaccination rates. The big picture: The U.S. has reported at least 2,318 measles cases as of late last week — a 35-year record. The majority of cases are among unvaccinated people. None of Pennsylvania's cases this year have involved people who were fully vaccinated. By the numbers: Pennsylvania has reported 142 measles cases through yesterday — far surpassing last year's total (16). That's the state's highest annual total in at least 25 years. The previous high was 17 cases in 2019, the state health department tells Axios. Pennsylvania has recorded 26 hospitalizations and no deaths this year. Zoom in: Lancaster County is the epicenter of Pennsylvania's outbreak, accounting for roughly 60% of cases. The county also has some of the state's lowest student vaccination rates. Other measles hotspots include Lebanon County (20 cases) and Chester County (14 cases). Meanwhile, Philadelphia, Delaware and Bucks counties have no reported cases. Between the lines: Pennsylvania's student vaccination rate was estimated at 92.4% last year, per the CDC — below the herd immunity threshold of 95% that public health authorities say is necessary to contain the virus' spread. Zoom out: The U.S. has confirmed measles cases across 45 jurisdictions —South Carolina, Utah, Texas, Florida and Arizona have the most reported cases. The last time the U.S. recorded more measles cases was in 1991, with 9,643 cases, according to CDC data. Threat level: Measles symptoms include a cough, high fever and conjunctivitis as well as a telltale rash. But symptoms can escalate into complications such as severe ear infections, pneumonia and encephalitis or inflammation of the brain that can lead to permanent brain damage and even death, per Johns Hopkins Medicine. Scale, impact and risk
Pennsylvania has reported 142 measles cases (compared to 16 in 2023) and 26 hospitalizations, with no deaths. has reported at least 2,318 cases as of late last week, a 35-year record. The majority of cases are among unvaccinated people. Pennsylvania's student vaccination rate was 92.4% in 2025, below the 95% herd immunity threshold.
Response, status and uncertainty
Pennsylvania's health department has launched an online dashboard to track measles cases. The outbreak is linked to lower vaccination rates in affected communities. No deaths have been reported in Pennsylvania. The source is a full original report from Axios.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Scientists find clusters of measles vulnerability in Northern California - Kaiser Permanente Division of Research

News Brief
Kaiser Permanente’s Northern California Division of Research released a study on July 30, 2026, revealing clusters of measles vulnerability across specific census tracts in the region. The analysis shows that vaccination coverage in these areas falls below the herd immunity threshold, creating a heightened risk for measles outbreaks. While the report confirms the existence of low‐coverage pockets, it does not provide case or death statistics, nor does it outline immediate public health interventions. The findings suggest a need for further investigation and potentially targeted vaccination campaigns to close coverage gaps and mitigate outbreak risk. The study is an official scientific assessment, offering a data‐driven basis for future public health planning.
Original source text (English)

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Heartland health official discusses back-to-school immunizations as measles cases rise - KFVS12

News Brief
As the back-to-school season approaches in Cape Girardeau County, Missouri, health officials are urging parents to ensure their children are vaccinated against measles, following a surge in U.S. cases. The Cape Girardeau County Public Health Center reports that measles cases in 2026 have exceeded 2,300, the highest number ever recorded in the country. The virus is highly contagious and airborne, making vaccination critical. The center offers MMR vaccinations during regular hours and has introduced a drive-through clinic on Wednesdays for convenience. Health officials emphasize that routine checkups, including physicals, are essential to detect early health issues in students, particularly young athletes. Parents are encouraged to schedule appointments to ensure their children are fully prepared for the school year.

By Rachel Grubbs Published: Jul. 30, 2026 at 10:12 PM UTC|Updated: 23 hours ago Share Add Us On GoogleAdd as a preferred source on Google CAPE GIRARDEAU, Mo. (KFVS) - In less than a month, Heartland students will be back in classrooms. As parents check off items on back-to-school shopping lists, health officials want them to remember to squeeze in routine checkups. That includes making sure students are up to date on vaccinations. It’s something Illinois and Missouri both require before children go to school, including immunization against measles. CDC officials say there have been more reported measles cases so far in 2026 than all of last year. It’s a record-breaking number, more than 2300. It’s also the highest number reported in the U.S. in decades. Amber Grant with the Cape Girardeau County Public Health Center said the measles virus is highly contagious. “It is airborne, so just being in the room with somebody and breathing the same air causes a chance for that person to be able to contract that illness.” People can receive vaccinations, including the MMR vaccine, through the Cape County Public Health Center. They’re offered Monday through Friday during open hours, 8:30 a.m. to 4 p.m. New this year, Grant said they’re offering a drive-through vaccine clinic on Wednesdays. “They do not even have to get out of their car. They just drive around. We’re able to review that immunization record and discuss with them what vaccines they’re due for and then go from there.” Grant said physicals are a chance to catch things early, including abnormalities with hearing or vision and curves in the spine. It’s especially important for young athletes, who may need injury accommodation. “In addition, they can listen to their heart, their lungs, see if there’s any kind of concerns there that might require further followup. They can kind of get just a good head-to-toe assessment to see if there’s any concerns that they need to think about for that school year.” The public health center is located 1121 Linden St. in Cape Girardeau. Call (573) 335-7846 to schedule an appointment. Copyright 2026 KFVS. All rights reserved. 麻疹 在美国卷土重来令人“担忧” - Spectrum News

Measles makes 'concerning' resurgence in the U.S. - Spectrum News
新闻简报麻疹 在美国再次出现令人担忧的情况,CDC 报告 2026 年确诊病例为 2,318 例,为 35 年来的最高数字。暴发 特别影响未接种疫苗的社区,其原因是错误信息和 疫苗接种 率下降。虽然没有死亡报告,但 麻疹 仍然是一种高度传染性和潜在致命的疾病,能够引起肺炎、脑损伤和癫痫等严重并发症。包括纽约州立大学上州医科大学的肖博士在内的医学专家敦促家长确保孩子接种疫苗,并咨询医疗保健提供者以解决问题。这种复苏尤其令人担忧,因为许多年轻的医疗保健专业人员和家长从未遇到过 麻疹,这可能导致 诊断 延迟和进一步的 传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-29。除了持续的复苏之外,未指定事件时间表。
地点与对象
美国,特别提到纽约(纽约市以外)。受影响的人群包括儿童和未接种疫苗的人。
事件
麻疹 在美国正在经历令人担忧的复苏,CDC 报告确诊病例已达 2,318 例,创下 35 年来的最高数字。暴发 归因于错误信息和缺乏 疫苗接种。
规模、影响与风险
全国确诊病例2,318例(35年来最高)。纽约市以外的地区报告了九例病例。目前尚无死亡报告,但 麻疹 可能会导致严重的并发症,包括肺炎、脑损伤、癫痫发作和死亡。该病毒具有高度传染性,并在未接种疫苗的社区传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
医疗专业人员建议家长确保儿童接种疫苗。纽约州立大学上州医科大学的 Shaw 强调了 疫苗接种 的重要性,并敦促家长咨询医疗保健提供者。由于公众对 疫苗 的信心下降,以及以前从未见过 麻疹 的年轻医疗保健专业人员缺乏认识,这种死灰复燃的现象令人担忧。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles makes 'concerning' resurgence in the U.S. - Spectrum News

News Brief
Measles is making a concerning resurgence in the United States, with the CDC reporting 2,318 confirmed cases in 2026, the highest number in 35 years. The outbreak, particularly affecting unvaccinated communities, is driven by misinformation and declining vaccination rates. While no deaths have been reported, measles remains a highly contagious and potentially fatal disease, capable of causing severe complications like pneumonia, brain damage, and seizures. Medical experts, including Dr. Shaw from SUNY Upstate Medical University, are urging parents to ensure their children are vaccinated and to consult healthcare providers to address concerns. The resurgence is especially alarming as many younger healthcare professionals and parents have never encountered measles, potentially leading to delayed diagnosis and further transmission.
Original source text (English)
ROCHESTER, N.Y. — First day of school is just right around the corner, and medical professionals are advising parents to not overlook an important step before walking the halls at schools — vaccines. What You Need To Know Measles is one of the most contagious viruses in the country — affecting anyone, but most commonly within children As medical experts report the virus surpasses an alarming benchmark, measles has made its resurgence and is having doctors become more worrisome this time around There have been nine reported cases of measles in New York outside of New York City so far this year For parent Jennifer Rodriguez, she is getting a head start on preparing her son for the back-to-school season from social skills to playtime and most importantly staying vigilant on the most up to date immunizations. “Jacob is going to be 14 months old in a couple days,” Rodriguez said. “Like every other parent, first-time parents, we're always worried about everything, especially vaccines. So, I always tend to ask my pediatrician how safe it is. And he always reassures me that it's safe and that they're very important, especially during the winter and the pneumonia, COVID [and] flu. So he has all his shots, so he is healthy. And yeah, all the shots are up to date. As a parent, why is it so important for your kids to have vaccines, not only for his own protection, but also for, like, other kids, I really believe in the science.” Measles has continued a historic resurgence across the United States. “I was like terrified because I always want to bring him outside [and] have him play with other kids [and] socialize,” Rodriguez said. “But you just never know. Especially how bad of a virus measles is like. It can cause brain damage or even death. Especially when you see the recent like rising cases because of people not wanting to vaccinate recently. And it's very scary, especially when he grows up and starts going to school, being around other kids. And I just want him to be as protected as he can.” Going into its record breaking second year in a row, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reports there has been 2,318 confirmed cases across the country, marking its highest number in 35 years. “It is very concerning for me as an infectious disease specialist, as a pediatrician, as a mother of children, and as someone who really cares and worries about the community I live in,” SUNY Upstate Medical University pediatrics professor Dr. Jana Shaw said. Medical professionals say the alarming cases are due to misinformation and lack of vaccinations. “Confidence in vaccination among public declines is misinformation or disinformation, essentially intentional spread of false information about vaccines,” Shaw said. “The reason why this is worrisome is unlike, for some, measles may be perceived as a mild infection. And just the opposite is true. Measles is a very serious childhood infection. It's a virus that is highly contagious. It will find children and adults who are not immune. The infection occurs, rather quickly, after exposure. And those symptoms may not be initially recognized by healthcare professionals because we been lucky and fortunate to see measles essentially eliminated in the United States since 2000. But what it also means [is] that most of the younger healthcare professionals and parents have not seen measles, and as such, they may not be aware of the infection and may not think of the infection, which further leads to propagation transmission in our communities.” And although no deaths have been reported so far, the disease can still be fatal. “The reason why we vaccinate is because we don't know ahead of time which children or adults will go and develop serious complications pneumonia, dehydration, brain infection, seizures [or] death,” Shaw said. “We don't know ahead of time. Even healthy children die from measles. Sometimes they may go on developing seizures that leads to a vegetative state. There's no cure for this condition and is fatal. It kills all the people who end up with this complication. So I'm mentioning all those details not to alarm or scare anyone, but to simply highlight that measles is not a benign or an infection. Not to fear. In fact, it's an infection that can have complications, can be very serious, can land children into hospital, and can also kill. And as such, it's really important that as parents, when they choose not to vaccinate, they understand that risk because unfortunately, they often see parent's inability to sort of weigh the risk and benefit of vaccination as the most effective preventive measure.” It comes as parents like Rodriguez hope to protect her child and possibly thousands of others. “If there is something that I can prevent from my child having, especially if it's like the illness, like the measles, I will do everything in my power to be able to help him with that and prevent that from happening to him,” Rodriguez said. As this infection continues to race through pockets of unvaccinated communities, Shaw is highly advising parents to speak with their medical provider about their options, the best time frame to receive a vaccine and walk through any anxieties they may have surrounded this matter to avoid further risk later down the line

Published on 2026-07-29. Event timeframe not specified beyond the ongoing resurgence.

United States, with specific mention of New York (outside NYC). Affected population includes children and unvaccinated individuals.
Event
Measles is experiencing a concerning resurgence in the U.S., with 2,318 confirmed cases reported by the CDC, marking the highest number in 35 years. The outbreak is attributed to misinformation and lack of vaccinations.
Scale, impact and risk
2,318 confirmed cases nationwide (highest in 35 years). Nine cases reported in New York outside NYC. No deaths reported, but measles can cause serious complications including pneumonia, brain damage, seizures, and death. The virus is highly contagious and spreads in unvaccinated communities.
Response, status and uncertainty
Medical professionals are advising parents to ensure children are vaccinated. Shaw from SUNY Upstate Medical University emphasizes the importance of vaccination and urges parents to consult healthcare providers. The resurgence is concerning due to declining public confidence in vaccines and lack of awareness among younger healthcare professionals who haven't seen measles before.

Measles detected in Anchorage wastewater - Alaska's News Source

News Brief
Traces of the measles virus have been detected in Anchorage wastewater over July 2026, indicating potential community transmission. The finding, confirmed through laboratory testing of wastewater samples, signals possible presence of the virus in Anchorage, though no specific case numbers or health impacts have been reported. Public health authorities have not yet disclosed details about ongoing investigations, response measures, or risk assessments. The detection underscores the utility of wastewater surveillance as an early warning tool for infectious diseases, though the full extent of the potential outbreak remains unclear pending further information.

4 名蒙哥马利县居民感染 麻疹 后,德克萨斯州卫生部门发出警报

Texas health issues alert after 4 Montgomery County residents infected with measles
新闻简报蒙哥马利县四名未接种疫苗的家庭成员被来自另一个州的旅行者感染后,德克萨斯州卫生官员发布了 麻疹 警报。这种高度传染性的病毒会带来更多病例的风险,特别是在未接种疫苗的个体中。卫生当局敦促居民接种 MMR 疫苗 疫苗,该疫苗的有效率超过 97%。症状包括发烧、咳嗽、流鼻涕和皮疹,感染者的传染性会持续几天。公共卫生措施包括建议暴露者在寻求护理之前联系医疗服务提供者,以最大程度地减少传播。目前尚未有死亡报告,但有可能出现肺炎和脑部炎症等并发症。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026 年 7 月 29 日。事件日期未明确报告,但在一名携带​​ 麻疹 的旅行者进入德克萨斯州后确认了病例。
地点与对象
德克萨斯州蒙哥马利县。受影响人群:四名未接种疫苗的家庭成员。
事件
在蒙哥马利县一个未接种疫苗的家庭中确认了 麻疹 暴发。在这家人被来自另一个州的 麻疹 旅行者感染后,德克萨斯州卫生服务部 (DSHS) 发布了健康警报。
规模、影响与风险
确诊病例四例。麻疹 被描述为最具传染性的病毒之一,官员们表示可能会发生更多病例,特别是在未接种疫苗的人群中。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在敦促 疫苗接种 与 MMR 疫苗 一起使用,该 MMR 疫苗 可通过医疗保健提供者和免费/低成本计划获得。建议患有 麻疹 的人待在家里,接触过的人应在寻求护理之前致电医疗保健提供者,以降低 传播 风险。该病毒可以在空气中停留长达两个小时。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Texas health issues alert after 4 Montgomery County residents infected with measles

News Brief
Texas health officials have issued a measles alert after four unvaccinated family members in Montgomery County were infected by a traveler from another state. The highly contagious virus poses a risk of additional cases, particularly among unvaccinated individuals. Health authorities are urging residents to get vaccinated with the MMR vaccine, which is over 97% effective. Symptoms include fever, cough, runny nose, and a rash, and infected individuals are contagious for several days. Public health measures include advising exposed individuals to contact providers before seeking care to minimize spread. No deaths have been reported, but complications like pneumonia and brain inflammation are possible.
Original source text (English)

Here’s what drivers need to know Barbecue Inn is closing — but its real story is the generations of Houstonians who made it a tradition Close Local News Texas health issues alert after 4 Montgomery County residents infected with measles Brittany Taylor, Senior Digital Content Producer Published: July 29, 2026 at 12:18 PM Tags: Texas Vaccines for Children, measles prevention, Texas Department of State Health Services, measles health alert, public health alert Texas Measles (CDC) HOUSTON – Texas health officials are warning residents and health care providers to stay alert after four cases of measles were confirmed in an unvaccinated Montgomery County family. The Texas Department of State Health Services (DSHS) issued a health alert after the family members were infected by a person with measles who traveled to Texas from another state. Recommended Videos Because measles is one of the most contagious viruses, state health officials said additional cases could occur, particularly among people who are not vaccinated. “Because measles is so contagious, additional cases could occur among unvaccinated people in the area,” DSHS said in the alert. Health officials are urging Texans to make sure they are protected by getting vaccinated with the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine. According to DSHS, two doses of the MMR vaccine are more than 97% effective at preventing measles. The vaccine is available through health care providers, and free or low-cost options are offered for eligible families through the Texas Vaccines for Children program. Pharmacists can administer the vaccine without a prescription to people 14 and older and with a prescription for children 13 and younger. What are the symptoms of measles? Symptoms typically appear seven to 21 days after exposure and often begin with: High fever Cough Runny nose Red, watery eyes A few days later, a red rash usually develops on the face before spreading to the neck, trunk and the rest of the body. People with measles are contagious from about four days before the rash appears until four days after it develops. Anyone who suspects they have measles should stay home during that time to avoid spreading the virus. What should you do if you’ve been exposed? Health officials recommend calling your health care provider before going to a doctor’s office or clinic if you believe you’ve been exposed to measles. Calling ahead allows providers to arrange testing while reducing the risk of exposing other patients. Measles spreads through coughing, sneezing or simply being near an infected person. The virus can remain in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves an area. While many people recover, measles can cause serious complications, including pneumonia, brain inflammation and, in some cases, death. DSHS said people with measles should seek emergency medical care if they develop difficulty breathing, severe dehydration, confusion, weakness, decreased alertness or other sudden changes in behavior. For young children, warning signs include a blue color around the mouth, crying without producing tears, severe loss of appetite or unusually low energy. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Clark County Public Health warns of possible measles exposure - The Reflector

News Brief
Clark County Public Health has issued a warning regarding potential measles exposure after a child with a confirmed case visited The Vancouver Clinic's Salmon Creek 1 Clinic. The alert, published on July 29, 2026, informs the public about possible exposure to the highly contagious virus. While only one confirmed case has been reported, health officials are urging potentially exposed individuals to monitor for symptoms and follow guidance. The clinic location in Salmon Creek, Clark County, is specified as the site of potential exposure. No further details about the investigation or response measures are currently available.

State on alert after measles detected in Anchorage - Chilkat Valley News

News Brief
State health officials in Alaska are on alert after the measles virus was detected in Anchorage. The Alaska Department of Epidemiology has issued a warning to the public following the confirmed case. No further details on the number of cases, potential exposures, or the specific response measures have been released at this time. The detection underscores the importance of vaccination and public health vigilance against highly contagious diseases like measles.
